p&0&p\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}^{s_{3}}&\mathcal{P}^{s_{2}}&\mathcal{D}^{\ell_{2}}\\
p&p&0\end{bmatrix}

where
\sum_{n=1}^{4}\beta_{n}=\beta
. This is the same 4-point function as found in
BMV2018
;
BMV2019
;
MTV2017
. It can be noted from the above computation that each Euclidean length
\beta_{n}
between each vertex contributes a factor
e^{-\beta_{n}\mathcal{C}(s_{n})}
, that each vertex with the boundary contributes a 3j-symbol, and that each crossing contributes a 6j-symbol. This is, in fact, more generally true, providing one with Feynman rules to compute general

-point functions in terms of the Casimir eigenvalues, and 3j- and 6j-symbols
MTV2017
.

28

See e.g. a recent preprint
CuiRozali2025splittinggluingsinedilatongravity
where interesting relations for 6j-symbols were obtained from analysing such glueing rules for higher-point functions, also in the
q
-deformed context.
Analogous Feynman rules were derived for the DSSYK model in
BINT2019
. They therefore have a very similar representation-theoretical interpretation, but now adapted for the quantum group. An important change one should be aware of here is that the tensor product becomes braided (see e.g.
QGAR
;
KasselQuantumGroups
;
CPAGtQG
), and that the product of matrix elements is no longer commutative. In Section
4.2
, we will follow similar steps as in this section to revisit the calculation of the partition and

-analogue of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
.

3
The locally compact quantum group

\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}

It was directly calculated in
BINT2019
that the
n
-point functions for the double-scaled SYK model
K2015
are given in terms of 3j- and 6j-symbols corresponding to the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
. This suggests that, in line with the previous section, one can describe the double-scaled SYK model through the representation theory of the quantum group. Several attempts for such a description have been made
BINN2023
;
BM2024
;
BMT2025
;
HVX2025QuantumSymmetry
, but none take advantage of the von Neumann algebraic description and the corresponding regular representations of the quantum group. Therefore, we will improve on this by considering the von Neumann algebraic description, as introduced by Kustermans and Vaes
KV2000
;
KV2000_1
;
KV2003
(see also
CK2010
;
TItQGaD
;
D2014
for overviews), of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
, which was already conjectured in
BINT2019
to provide the appropriate representation-theoretic framework for describing the dynamics of the model. Importantly, as will be discussed in Section
3.1
, this quantum group does not allow for a locally compact description, and one instead has to extend to its normaliser in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. The von Neumann algebraic quantum group for this normaliser has been constructed in
KK2003
, and its dual in
GKK2010
. We will discuss this construction in Section
3.2
and discuss its regular representations in Section
3.3
. Then, in Section
3.4
, we construct its quantum Gauss decomposition and we derive the action of the Casimir operator in this decomposition in Section
3.5
.

3.1
The quantum coordinate algebra

As is usual in non-commutative geometry, a deformation of a locally compact space is described through a deformation of the function algebra over that space. In this sense, a quantum group is defined as a deformation of the Hopf algebra of functions on a Lie group. In particular, consider the deformation parameter
0<q<1
, and define the
quantum coordinate algebra

\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
to be the Hopf
*
-algebra generated by
\alpha
and
\gamma
with relations
QGAR

\alpha\gamma=q\gamma\alpha,\quad\alpha\gamma^{*}=q\gamma^{*}\alpha,\quad\gamma\gamma^{*}=\gamma^{*}\gamma,\quad\alpha\alpha^{*}-q^{2}\gamma^{*}\gamma=1=\alpha^{*}\alpha-\gamma^{*}\gamma,

(46)

with a coproduct given by

\Delta(\alpha)=\alpha\otimes\alpha+q\gamma^{*}\otimes\gamma,\quad\Delta(\gamma)=\gamma\otimes\alpha+\alpha^{*}\otimes\gamma,

(47)

and a counit
\varepsilon(\alpha)=1
,
\varepsilon(\gamma)=0
and antipode
S(\alpha)=\alpha^{*}
,
S(\gamma)=-q\gamma
. It can be seen that this algebra is now non-commutative, and reduces to the classical coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
in the limit
q\to 1
. In the classical case, the generators
\alpha
and
\gamma
are functions
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\to\mathbb{C}
as defined in (
2
). Therefore, for the quantum algebra, one could interpret
\alpha
and
\gamma
similarly as non-commuting functions over the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
. Similar to the classical case, the coproduct, counit, and antipode in the coordinate algebra can be thought of as describing the multiplication, unit, and inverse on
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
.

Similarly, there is a deformation of the universal enveloping algebra. In particular, the
quantum universal enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
is the (associative, unital) algebra generated by
E,F,K
and
K^{-1}
subject to relations
QGAR

KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1,\quad KE=qEK,\quad KF=q^{-1}FK,\quad EF-FE=\frac{K^{2}-K^{-2}}{q-q^{-1}}

(48)

and with the
*
-structure
K^{*}=K
and
E^{*}=-F
. Moreover, there is a natural Hopf algebra structure with coproduct
\Delta
, counit
\varepsilon
and antipode
S
given by

\begin{gathered}\Delta(K)=K\otimes K,\quad\Delta(E)=K\otimes E+E\otimes K^{-1},\quad\Delta(F)=K\otimes F+F\otimes K^{-1}\\
S(K)=K^{-1},\quad S(E)=-q^{-1}E,\quad S(F)=-qF,\quad\varepsilon(K)=1,\quad\varepsilon(E)=\varepsilon(F)=0.\end{gathered}

(49)

Here, it can be noted that the quantum universal enveloping algebra reduces to the classical one by defining
K=q^{H/2}
and taking the limit
q\to 1
. Therefore, the Cartan element
K
corresponds to an exponent of
H
, and is group-like
29
29

29

A group-like element of a Hopf algebra is an element
x
such that the coproduct is given by
\Delta(x)=x\otimes x
.
. The Casimir element, i.e. generator of the centre, for the quantum universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
is given by

\Omega\coloneq\frac{q^{-1}K^{-2}+qK^{2}-2}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}+FE.

(50)

Similar to the classical case, there exists a non-degenerate dual pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{U}_{q}\times\mathcal{A}_{q}\to\mathbb{C}
which satisfies the equalities (
8
), and is given by
K1993

\langle K,\alpha\rangle=\sqrt{q},\quad\langle K,\gamma\rangle=0,\quad\langle E,\alpha\rangle=0,\quad\langle E,\gamma\rangle=0,\quad\langle F,\alpha\rangle=0,\quad\langle F,\gamma\rangle=1.

(51)

Moreover, this duality induces a left and right action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}
on
\mathcal{A}_{q}
through (
9
). In the classical case, these actions corresponded to taking derivatives along the direction of the Lie algebra elements, and we therefore interpret the action of the deformed algebra in a similar way, as ‘taking derivatives’ on the quantum group manifold
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
.

The description is, of course, again purely algebraic, and we would like to describe its analytical properties as well. In particular, as in the classical case, we would like to upgrade this algebra to a von Neumann algebra, which can be thought of as describing non-commutative measure theory. In the classical case, the commutative von Neumann algebra was given by essentially bounded functions on the corresponding space. In the quantum case, a natural choice would be a (non-commutative) von Neumann algebra that is generated by
\alpha
and
\gamma
. To obtain such a von Neumann algebra, the algebraic objects need to be upgraded to operators, which can be achieved by considering representations of the coordinate algebra. It turns out, however, that these representations are not actually compatible with the coproduct
W1991
. To gain some intuition behind this problem and its solution, we will still construct and discuss these representations first.

We define the
q
-interval

[0,\infty)_{q}\coloneq\{q^{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and consider the Hilbert space
L^{2}_{q}([0,\infty))
of ‘square-integrable’ functions
f\colon[0,\infty)_{q}\to\mathbb{C}
with inner product given by the
q
-integral

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{f(x)}g(x)d_{q}x\coloneq(1-q)\sum_{x\in[0,\infty)_{q}}x\overline{f(x)}g(x),

(52)

which reduces to the ordinary integral in the limit
q\to 1
. On this Hilbert space, there is an orthonormal basis
\{\delta_{p}\}_{p\in[0,\infty)_{q}}
, where
\delta_{p}
is the function given by

\delta_{p}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-q)p}}\delta_{p,x}\quad\text{for }x\in[0,\infty)_{q},

(53)

and can be interpreted as a
q
-deformation of the Dirac-delta function. Let us now construct the irreducible representations of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
on this Hilbert space. Since the group is non-compact, the actions of the algebra generators will generally be given by unbounded operators. Therefore, the representation is only well-defined on some dense subset of
L^{2}_{q}([0,\infty))
, which for our purposes will be given by all functions
[0,\infty)_{q}\to\mathbb{C}
which are non-zero on only finitely many basis elements, i.e. are compactly supported. For
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, there are (mutually inequivalent) irreducible
*
-representations
\pi_{\theta}
of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
on the compactly supported functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\theta}\coloneq L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
given by
W1991

\pi_{\theta}(\alpha)\delta_{x}=e^{i\theta}\sqrt{1+x}~\delta_{q^{-2}x},\quad\pi_{\theta}(\gamma)\delta_{x}=e^{i\theta}\sqrt{x}~\delta_{x}

(54)

for
x\in[0,\infty)_{q^{2}}
.
30
30

30

Note here that a representation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
is defined on the space of functions over the
q^{2}
-interval. This inconsistency in the power of
q
is due to a historical difference in convention between special functions and quantum groups. Moreover, we only consider representations such that the spectrum of the spherical element is given by
\sigma(\gamma^{*}\gamma)=q^{2\mathbb{Z}}
. In principle, there is a bigger family of representations with
\sigma(\gamma^{*}\gamma)=q^{2\beta}q^{2\mathbb{Z}}
for any
\beta\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, where
\beta
corresponds to a character of the fundamental group of the corresponding symplectic leaf
\Sigma_{\theta}
(
56
)
K1993
.
Here, we choose a particular convention (which one might call the ‘polarisation’) for the representations, requiring that
(\pi_{\theta}(\gamma^{*}\gamma)f)(x)=xf(x)
for a function
f\in L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
. Therefore, one may interpret
\gamma^{*}\gamma
as a position operator. Moreover, it can be verified that this operator is invariant under the left and right action of the Cartan element
K
, i.e.
K\triangleright\gamma^{*}\gamma=\gamma^{*}\gamma=\gamma^{*}\gamma\triangleleft K
. In particular, this element generates the
*
-subalgebra of left and right
K
-invariant elements, i.e.
31
31

31

Here,
\langle\gamma^{*}\gamma\rangle
denotes the algebra generated by
\gamma^{*}\gamma
, i.e. all polynomials in
\gamma^{*}\gamma
with complex coefficients.

\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{U}(1)\backslash\mathrm{SU}(1,1)/\mathrm{U}(1))=\langle\gamma^{*}\gamma\rangle.

(55)

Therefore, the position operator
\gamma^{*}\gamma
is a
spherical element
for the Cartan decomposition of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
.

These representations can be interpreted to be obtained from geometric quantisation of a symplectic leaf of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
. Indeed, the deformation of the coordinate algebra (
46
) corresponds to a (formal) deformation of the Poisson structure on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
, i.e. a commutator bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
such that
h^{-1}[a,b]\text{ mod }h=\{a\text{ mod }h,b\text{ mod }h\}
for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes\mathbb{C}[[h]]
and
q=\exp(-h)
, where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
denotes the ‘standard’ Poisson bracket on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)

K1994
. The
h\to 0
limit of the kernel of an irreducible representation then corresponds to a maximal Poisson ideal, and therefore a symplectic leaf, of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)

LevendorskiiSoibelman1991
;
VaksmanSoibelman1988
, giving a one-to-one correspondence between the symplectic leaves and the irreducible representations of the quantum coordinate algebra. For
\pi_{\theta}
, this kernel is given by
\ker\pi_{\theta}=\langle\gamma-e^{2i\theta}\gamma^{*}\rangle
, with the corresponding symplectic leaf consisting of the elements in
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
with
\text{arg }\gamma=\theta
, i.e.

\Sigma_{\theta}\coloneq\left\{\begin{bmatrix}e^{i\varphi}\cosh\frac{\rho}{2}&e^{-i\theta}\sinh\frac{\rho}{2}\\
e^{i\theta}\sinh\frac{\rho}{2}&e^{-i\varphi}\cosh\frac{\rho}{2}\end{bmatrix}:\varphi\in[0,2\pi)\text{ and }\rho>0\right\}\subset\mathrm{SU}(1,1).

(56)

The geometric interpretation of the representation
\pi_{\theta}
is now as follows. Firstly, it can be interpreted as a geometric quantisation (see e.g.
WoodhouseGeometricQuantisation
) of the symplectic leaf
\Sigma_{\theta}
, with the functions
\alpha,\gamma
acting as operators on the prequantum Hilbert space, and a chosen polarisation such that
\gamma^{*}\gamma
acts as the position operator. In addition, the symplectic leaf itself, and therefore the coordinates, are quantised, i.e.
\sinh\rho/2\in q^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus, the representations
\pi_{\theta}
of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
correspond to a ‘double quantisation’ (that is, quantisation along with a
q
-deformation) of the two-dimensional symplectic leaves of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
. Note here an important difference with the classical case: the irreducible
*
-representations of the classical coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}(\textrm{SU}(1,1))
are all one-dimensional, corresponding to evaluations at a given point, whereas the irreducible
*
-representations of the quantised coordinate algebra are infinite-dimensional. We see that, in this optics,
q
-deformation tends to glue ordinary points into non-local ‘fat points’, corresponding to infinite-dimensional irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra.

Let us now comment on the incompatibility of the representations on the level of the Hopf algebra. In particular, one could ask whether
(\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\varphi})\circ\Delta
is again a well-defined representation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
, that is, whether there exists a dense subset of the Hilbert space that is closed under the action of the coordinate algebra. Such a dense subset usually corresponds to certain boundary conditions on the functions. Therefore, we could equivalently say that the action of the coordinate algebra should not deform the boundary conditions. Since, according to the choice we made at the start, we consider functions over the spectrum of the position operator
\gamma^{*}\gamma
, these boundary conditions correspond to self-adjoint extensions of
((\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\varphi})\circ\Delta)(\gamma^{*}\gamma)
. This operator is given by a doubly-infinite Jacobi operator and its self-adjoint extensions are parametrised by an angle
\psi\in[0,2\pi)

K2001
, with corresponding boundary conditions given by
K1994

\mathcal{D}_{\psi}=\{f\in L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty)):\cos{\psi}\cdot f(\infty)+\sin{\psi}\cdot(D_{q^{2}}f)(\infty)=0\},

(57)

where
f(\infty)\coloneq\lim_{n\to\infty}f(q^{-2n})
and
(D_{q^{2}}f)(x)\coloneq\frac{f(x)-f(qx)}{x-qx}
is the
q
-derivative. Unfortunately, these boundary conditions are deformed by the action of other elements in the coordinate algebra, for example,
(\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\phi})(\Delta(\alpha))(\mathcal{D}_{\psi})\not\subset\mathcal{D}_{\psi}
. Therefore, the symmetric operator
\gamma^{*}\gamma
cannot be extended to a self-adjoint operator in a way that is compatible with the action of
\alpha
, making the tensor product of representations ill-defined.
32
32

32

It would be interesting to understand a stringy interpretation of this argument better, e.g. in terms of brane boundary conditions becoming ‘fuzzy’ upon
q
-deformation.
This is the essence of the no-go theorem by Woronowicz
W1991
, which has the consequence that the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
does not exist as a locally compact quantum group. This inconsistency can be regarded as a problem with defining the group multiplication on the naive quantum group manifold
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
.

As pointed out in
K1994
, the symplectic geometric interpretation of this negative result is the fact that the local
dressing action
33
33

33

Recall that the Poisson structure on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
induces a Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
can be realised as the Lie algebra of left-invariant differential 1-forms on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
. The bundle map
B\colon T^{*}\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\to T\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
then induces the linear map
\lambda\colon\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
, where
\lambda(\xi)
is referred to as the left dressing vector field corresponding to
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Integrating
\lambda
gives a local action
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)^{*}
of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
, which is called the left
local dressing action

CPAGtQG
;
K1994
. The right (local) dressing action is defined analogously.
of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)^{*}
on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
cannot be extended to a global one. Explicitly, let
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\theta}
with basis
\{\delta^{*}_{x}\}
be the dual of
\mathcal{H}_{\theta}
such that
\langle\delta^{*}_{x},\delta_{y}\rangle=\delta_{x,y}
. We can define the dual representation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\theta}^{*}
via
\langle\pi^{*}(a)\delta^{*}_{x},\delta_{y}\rangle\coloneq\langle\delta^{*}_{x},\pi(S(a))\delta_{y}\rangle
for
a\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
. It can then be easily verified that
\pi^{*}_{\theta}\cong\pi_{\theta+\pi}
. In particular, for the tensor product of representations, we have
\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\theta+\pi}\cong\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi^{*}_{\theta}
, which defines an action on
\mathcal{H}_{\theta}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\theta}^{*}
corresponding to the quantum adjoint action and reduces to the local dressing action in the classical
q\to 1
limit
K1994
. The above negative result then corresponds to the fact that the local dressing action of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)^{*}
on
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
does not extend to a global one –
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\cdot\mathrm{SU}(1,1)^{*}
is not dense in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
– thus making the tensor product of representations ill-defined. The same argument can be extended to any tensor product of representations
\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\varphi}
.

The solution by Korogodsky
K1994
was to extend
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
to its normaliser in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. Namely, for this normaliser, the dressing action does exist globally, which therefore also suggests that the tensor product of irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra of this normaliser may be well-defined. Indeed, in such an extension, the boundary conditions (
57
) will instead be given by smoothness conditions, which may remain unaltered under the action of the coordinate algebra. Let us make this explicit. Recall from (
25
) that the normaliser of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is given by

\begin{split}\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}&=\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\cup\mathrm{SU}(1,1)v\\
&=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&e\bar{\gamma}\\
\gamma&e\bar{\alpha}\end{bmatrix}:\alpha,\gamma\in\mathbb{C},e\in\{\pm 1\}\text{ and }|\alpha|^{2}-|\gamma|^{2}=e\right\},\end{split}

(58)

where we have now explicitly written out both parts in the normaliser in matrix form, by introducing an extra variable
e\in\{\pm 1\}
. In particular, the connected component of the identity corresponds to
e=+1
, while the connected component of
v
corresponds to
e=-1
.

Thus, to quantise the coordinate algebra of the normaliser, we introduce an additional element
e
satisfying

e^{2}=1,\quad\Delta(e)=e\otimes e,\quad S(e)=e,\quad\epsilon(e)=1,\quad e^{*}=e

(59)

and
ae=ea
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1))
. We also define its dual pairing with the universal enveloping algebra as
\langle X,e\rangle=\varepsilon(X)
for
X\in\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
.
The coordinate algebra of the normaliser
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is then generated by
\alpha,\gamma,e
, subject to (
59
), and the following relations/comultiplication:
34
34

34

In particular, the coordinate algebra for the normaliser is obtained from the coordinate algebra of
\mathrm{SL}_{q}(2,\mathbb{C})
(together with the specified element
e
) by imposing the
*
-structure
\alpha^{*}=e\delta
,
\beta^{*}=qe\gamma
,
\gamma^{*}=q^{-1}e\beta
,
\delta^{*}=e\alpha
and
e^{*}=e

KK2003
.

\begin{gathered}\alpha\gamma=q\gamma\alpha,\quad\alpha\gamma^{*}=q\gamma^{*}\alpha,\quad\gamma\gamma^{*}=\gamma^{*}\gamma,\quad\alpha\alpha^{*}-q^{2}\gamma^{*}\gamma=e=\alpha^{*}\alpha-\gamma^{*}\gamma,\\
\Delta(\alpha)=\alpha\otimes\alpha+qe\gamma^{*}\otimes\gamma,\quad\Delta(\gamma)=\gamma\otimes\alpha+e\alpha^{*}\otimes\gamma.\end{gathered}

(60)

Moreover, the antipode becomes
S(\alpha)=e\alpha^{*}
and
S(\gamma)=-q\gamma
, while the counit remains the same.

The representations of this extended algebra now act on a function space with an extended domain. Namely, let us consider the
q
-interval
(-\infty,-1)_{q}\coloneq\{-q^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\}
and define
I_{q}=(-\infty,-1)_{q}\cup[0,\infty)_{q}
. We consider the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions
f\colon I_{q}\to\mathbb{C}
over this domain, i.e.
\mathcal{H}_{q}\coloneq L^{2}_{q}(I_{q})=L^{2}_{q}((-\infty,-1))\oplus L^{2}_{q}([0,\infty))
, with inner product

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{I_{q}}\overline{f(x)}g(x)d_{q}x\coloneq(1-q)\sum_{x\in I_{q}}|x|\overline{f(x)}g(x).

(61)

Similar to before, there is an orthonormal basis
\{\delta_{p}\}_{p\in I_{q}}
, where
\delta_{p}
is given by the function

\delta_{p}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-q)|p|}}\delta_{p,x}\quad\text{for }x\in I_{q}.

(62)

The irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
are now again defined on the compactly supported functions. In particular, for each
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, there is a
*
-representation of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on the compactly supported functions in
\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
given by
KK2003
35
35

35

We use a different convention than Koelink and Kustermans in
KK2003
. We get the same representation under the redefinition
\delta_{\pm q^{2k}}\to e^{ik\theta}\delta_{\pm q^{2k}}
and
x\to\mathrm{sgn}(p)p^{-2}
.

\pi_{\theta}(\alpha)\delta_{x}=e^{i\theta}\sqrt{\mathrm{sgn}(x)+|x|}~\delta_{q^{-2}x},\quad\pi_{\theta}(\gamma)\delta_{x}=e^{i\theta}\mathrm{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}~\delta_{x},\quad\pi_{\theta}(e)\delta_{x}=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\delta_{x},

(63)

for
x\in(-\infty,-1)_{q^{2}}\cup[0,\infty)_{q^{2}}
. If we restrict the action to just the functions in
L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
, we get back the same action as in (
54
). It is important to note that this representation is not irreducible, as
L^{2}_{q^{2}}((-\infty,-1))
and
L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
do not talk to each other through the action of the coordinate algebra. Also,
(\pi_{\theta}(e\gamma^{*}\gamma)f)(x)=xf(x)
for a function
f\in\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
, such that now
e\gamma^{*}\gamma
has become the position operator. This is also expected, as
\gamma^{*}\gamma
is a positive operator and thus can never have negative elements in its spectrum, which is resolved by the additional multiplication by
e
.

The coordinates
\theta
and
x
can be interpreted as coordinates on the
extended quantum Poincaré disk
. That is, we consider the points
z(x,\theta)\coloneq e^{i\theta}(1+x^{-1})^{-1/2}\in\mathbb{C}
for
x\in I_{q^{2}}
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, which are plotted in Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
The extended quantum Poincaré disk, where the points
z(x,\theta)\coloneq e^{i\theta}(1+x^{-1})^{-1/2}
for
x\in I_{q^{2}}
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
are plotted. The extension of the Poincaré disk is given in grey. This picture corresponds to a slicing through all the two-dimensional symplectic leaves of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Note that in the quantum case, the extended disk has a ‘maximal ring’. That is, the radial coordinate has a maximal value which corresponds to the end-point
-q^{-1}\in(-\infty,-1)_{q}
. The limits
x\to\infty
and
x\to-\infty
for
x\in I_{q^{2}}
correspond to the limit towards the unit circle
|z|=1
from the inside and outside of the disk, respectively. Also, in the
q\to 1
limit, the extended quantum Poincaré disk becomes dense in
\mathbb{C}
. We emphasise here that Figure
2
does not correspond to just one symplectic leaf of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. On the contrary, for every
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, the radial slice at that angle corresponds to one symplectic leaf of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
for
|z|<1
and one symplectic leaf of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)v
for
|z|>1
. Therefore, Figure
2
should be interpreted as a slice through all the 2-dimensional symplectic leaves of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, where the slicing is orthogonal to the coordinate
\varphi
as given in (
56
).

We would now like to verify that the above representations are indeed compatible with the coproduct. Since the representation is now defined on the space of functions over the spectrum of
e\gamma^{*}\gamma
, we should correspondingly consider the self-adjoint extensions of
(\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\varphi})(\Delta(e\gamma^{*}\gamma))
. This operator is a direct sum of an unbounded Jacobi operator and a doubly infinite Jacobi operator, which both have deficiency indices
(1,1)
. The deficiency indices of
(\pi_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\varphi})(\Delta(e\gamma^{*}\gamma))
are therefore
(2,2)
, which allows for more freedom in the choice of the self-adjoint extension, with the boundary conditions becoming ‘fuzzier’. In particular, there are now self-adjoint extensions parametrised by
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}
with smoothness conditions
KK2003

\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}=\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}:f(\infty)=\lambda f(-\infty)\text{ and }(\mathcal{D}_{q^{2}}f)(\infty))=\lambda(D_{q^{2}}f)(-\infty)\},

(64)

where
f(-\infty)\coloneq\lim_{n\to\infty}f(-q^{-2n})
. The important insight is now that the action of the quantum group
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
deforms the functions at
\infty
and
-\infty
in a similar way, such that the smoothness conditions (
64
) remain satisfied, rendering the tensor product of representations of the normaliser to be well-defined
K1994
. From Figure
2
, it can be noted that (
64
) corresponds to a (discretised version of a) smoothness condition along the unit circle, i.e. the boundary of the Poincaré disk, thus connecting the inner and outer disks along the Poincaré horizon.

3.2
The von Neumann algebraic quantum group

Now that we have well-defined representations of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, we are ready to introduce the corresponding locally compact quantum group, generalising (and
q
-deforming) the example of Section
2.1
. Different theories for locally compact quantum groups have been developed by Masuda, Nakagami and Woronowicz
MNW2003
, and by Kustermans and Vaes
KV2000
;
KV2000_1
. We will follow the approach of the latter, in which a locally compact quantum group is defined as follows: Consider a von Neumann algebra
M
together with a unital normal
*
-homomorphism
\Delta\colon M\to M\otimes M
such that
(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\Delta=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta
. If there, moreover, exist two normal, semi-finite, faithful (n.s.f.)
36
36

36

A n.s.f. weight is a map
\varphi\colon M^{+}\to[0,\infty]
that is
\sigma
-weakly lower semi-continuous (normal), such that
\mathcal{M}_{\varphi}^{+}=\{x\in M^{+}:\varphi(x)<\infty\}
generates
M
, i.e.
(\mathcal{M}_{\varphi}^{+})^{\prime}=M
(semi-finite), and
\varphi(x)=0
implies
x=0
(faithful)
CK2010
. One can think of it as a sufficiently well-behaved (e.g. with respect to taking limits) measure on our ‘non-commutative measure space’.
weights
\varphi,\psi
on
M
such that
37
37

37

Here,
M_{*}
denotes the predual of
M
(see footnote
6
).

\displaystyle\varphi((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(x))

\displaystyle=\varphi(x)\omega(1),\quad

\displaystyle\text{for all }\omega\in M_{*}^{+},x\in\mathcal{M}_{\varphi}^{+},\quad

\displaystyle\text{(left invariance)},

(65)

\displaystyle\psi((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta(x))

\displaystyle=\psi(x)\omega(1),\quad

\displaystyle\text{for all }\omega\in M_{*}^{+},x\in\mathcal{M}_{\psi}^{+},\quad

\displaystyle\text{(right invariance)},

then
(M,\Delta)
is called a
von Neumann algebraic quantum group
.
38
38

38

One might be surprised that there is no mention of the antipode and the counit in the definition of the von Neumann algebraic quantum group. However, it turns out that the antipode and counit can be obtained from the invariant Haar weights. Moreover, although the antipode
(Sx)(g)=x(g^{-1})
for the classical group is a bounded operator, this is not true in general. Namely, for general locally compact quantum groups, the antipode can only be defined on a
\sigma
-strong-
*
dense set, which can be considered as a slight weakening of the invertibility property for elements of a usual group
CK2010
.
Although this definition is rather technical, one can recognise the same ingredients as in Section
2.1
, where the von Neumann algebra was given by the essentially bounded functions and
\varphi
and
\psi
by the Haar weight. Since
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
is unimodular, one has
\varphi=\psi
, which, as we will see, remains true for the quantum deformation. For the rest of this paper, it is not necessary to understand all the technical details of this definition, so we will not discuss them. We refer to
KV2000
;
KV2000_1
;
KV2003
;
D2014
;
CK2010
;
TItQGaD
for full overviews.

For constructing the von Neumann algebraic quantum group of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we follow the work of Koelink and Kustermans
KK2003
. Firstly, it should be noted that the representations
\pi_{\theta}
given in (
63
) are too small to be faithful. Instead, we get a faithful representation by taking the direct integral
\pi=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pi_{\theta}d\theta

KS2001
. Geometrically, this gives a representation of the coordinate algebra over all the quantised 2-dimensional symplectic leaves of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The corresponding Hilbert space for this representation is then isomorphic to

\mathcal{H}\coloneq\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}.

(66)

Let
\{\zeta^{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the canonical orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. The faithful
*
-representation
\pi
of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on this Hilbert space can then be explicitly written as

\begin{split}\pi(\alpha)(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})&=\sqrt{\mathrm{sgn}(x)+|x|}~\zeta^{m+1}\otimes\delta_{q^{-2}x},\\
\pi(\gamma)(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})&=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}~\zeta^{m+1}\otimes\delta_{x},\\
\pi(e)(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})&=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x},\end{split}

(67)

on basis elements
\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
. To lighten up the notation a bit, in what follows we will not explicitly write
\pi
, but simply denote the action of an element
a\in\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on
\mathcal{H}
as
a.(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})\coloneq\pi(a)(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})
. One might now suggest that the appropriate von Neumann algebra for the quantum group is the one generated by
\alpha,\gamma
and
e
. However, the subspaces
L^{2}_{q^{2}}((-\infty,-1))
and
L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
form irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra, and, therefore, ‘do not talk’ to each other. This will have the consequence that the associated von Neumann algebra is not closed under comultiplication
KK2003
. Thus, in order to resolve this, we introduce an additional generator
u\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
given by

u.(\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x})=\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{-x},

(68)

where
\delta_{x}=0
if
x\not\in I_{q^{2}}
, which maps between
L^{2}_{q^{2}}((-\infty,-1))
and
L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
. It can be seen here that
u
plays a similar role for the quantum group as
v
has for the classical normaliser, since the action of
u
corresponds to a reflection with respect to
x=0
.

The von Neumann algebra of ‘essentially bounded operators’ on
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, denoted by
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, is now defined to be the von Neumann algebra on
\mathcal{H}
generated
39
39

39

Via the so called
affiliation construction
of Woronowicz
W1991
, which allows one to describe a von Neumann algebra that is ‘generated’ by unbounded operators.
by
\alpha,\gamma,e
and
u
. Explicitly, it is given by the commutant
40
40

40

The commutant of a subset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is the von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\coloneq\{x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}):xa=ax\text{ for all }a\in\mathcal{A}\}
. Moreover, let
A\colon\mathcal{D}(A)\to\mathcal{H}
and
B\colon\mathcal{D}(B)\to\mathcal{H}
be two (densely defined) unbounded operators, then
B
is an extension of
A
, denoted by
A\subseteq B
, if
\mathcal{D}(A)\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
and
B|_{\mathcal{D}(A)}=A
.

L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\coloneq\{A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}):Ax\subseteq xA\text{ and }Ax^{*}\subseteq x^{*}A\text{ for }x\in\{\alpha,\gamma,e,u\}\}^{\prime},

(69)

which can be shown to be isomorphic to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\otimes\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(I_{q^{2}}))

KK2003
. The important result by Koelink and Kustermans in that same paper is that there exists a coproduct

\Delta\colon L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\otimes L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})

(70)

which is well-defined and coassociative. For the purposes of this paper, we will not need the explicit (and quite bulky!) expression of this coproduct. It can be noted that this coproduct, when extended to the unbounded operators
\alpha,\gamma
and
e
, coincides with the coproduct on
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

For
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
n\equiv m\text{ mod }2
and
x\in I_{q^{2}}
such that
\pm q^{n+m}x\in I_{q^{2}}
, let us now define the map

\Phi^{\pm}(n,x,m)\zeta^{r}\otimes\delta_{y}=\delta_{\pm q^{n+m}x}(y)\zeta^{r+m}\otimes\delta_{x},

(71)

where
\delta_{p}
is the
q^{2}
-delta function as defined in (
62
). It turns out that these matrix elements form a (
\sigma
-weakly dense) topological basis for our von Neumann algebra
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})

KK2003
. The elements
\Phi^{-}(n,x,m)
correspond to operators that map between
L^{2}_{q^{2}}([0,\infty))
and
L^{2}_{q^{2}}((-\infty,-1))
. In the context of symplectic leaves as described around (
56
), these elements map between the quantisations of symplectic leaves of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
and
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)v
. As we will see in Section
4
, it’s exactly these elements that appear in the reduction to the double-scaled SYK model, and therefore also to Liouville QM in the
q\to 1
limit, which shows the point of considering the normaliser for treating these theories.

Following
KK2003
, we will now put the von Neumann algebra
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
(with the coproduct
\Delta
) into the framework of von Neumann algebraic quantum groups, by presenting the construction of an appropriate (left- and right-invariant) Haar weight, whose GNS space will reproduce the space of square-integrable functions with the basis introduced above (and, of course, with an action of
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
). First, we define the ‘square-integrable functions’ on
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as the Hilbert space
41
41

41

Note that the operators
\Phi^{\pm}(n,x,m)
correspond to maps
\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}\to\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
with an additional phase shift. The corresponding Hilbert space (after GNS construction) should reflect this same structure, and is therefore given by
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
. Additional intuition for why tensoring with another Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}
is needed here comes from comparing to how the classical
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
Lie group can be thought of as a circle fibration over the hyperbolic disk.

L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\coloneqq L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}.

(72)

For
n\equiv m\text{ mod }2
and
x\in I_{q^{2}}
such that
\pm q^{n+m}x\in I_{q^{2}}
, the elements

\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}\coloneq\zeta^{m}\otimes\delta_{x}\otimes\delta_{\pm q^{n+m}x},

(73)

form an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. That is, they satisfy

\langle\delta^{\varepsilon}_{n,x,m},\delta^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime},x^{\prime},m^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}.

(74)

Moving on to the constructing an appropriate Haar weight
\varphi
, we note that the trace on
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is given by
\text{Tr}(a)=\sum_{x\in I_{q^{2}}}\langle a.\zeta^{0}\otimes\delta_{x},\zeta^{0}\otimes\delta_{x}\rangle
.
The correct Haar weight that one needs for the construction is actually just a slight ‘twist’ of this trace, given by
42
42

42

Although the trace is already an n.s.f. weight, it is not left- and right-invariant and therefore not compatible with the additional symmetry. Unlike in the classical case, the correct Haar weight for the quantum group is therefore not tracial
KV2000_1
.

\varphi(a)\coloneq(1-q^{2})\text{Tr}(|\gamma|a|\gamma|),

(75)

which indeed was shown to be left- and right-invariant in
KK2003
. Let us now spell out the GNS construction for this Haar weight.
43
43

43

We remind that, according to the GNS construction for a n.s.f. weight
\varphi
on a von Neumann algebra
M
, the Hilbert space is constructed as the completion of
\{x\in M:\varphi(x^{*}x)<\infty\}/\{x\in M:\varphi(x^{*}x)=0\}
, where additionally, for a n.s.f (and thus faithful) weight, the ideal
\{x\in M:\varphi(x^{*}x)=0\}
is zero. In particular, this gives rise to a natural normal, non-degenerate and faithful
*
-representation of
M
, in addition to the natural GNS map
CK2010
. Note that the approach in
KK2003
is somewhat reversed: a Hilbert space is a priori given, and is then shown to correspond to a GNS construction for
\varphi
. This is the route we follow as well in our discussion of the GNS construction here.
That is, define:

(a)

The subspace
\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}=\{x\in L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}):\varphi(x^{*}x)<\infty\}
;

(b)

The linear map
\Lambda\colon\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}\to L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\ a\mapsto\sqrt{1-q^{2}}\sum_{x\in I_{q^{2}}}(a|\gamma|\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}}).\delta^{+}_{0,x,0}
;

(c)

The unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\colon L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathcal{B}(L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})),\ a\mapsto a\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{H}_{q^{2}}}
.

The triple
(L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\pi,\Lambda)
gives the GNS construction for the Haar weight
\varphi

KK2003
. That is, similar to Section
2.1
, it satisfies the following properties:

(a)

\Lambda(\mathcal{N}_{\varphi})
is dense in
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
;

(b)

\langle\Lambda(a),\Lambda(b)\rangle=\varphi(a^{*}b)
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}
;

(c)

\pi(x)\Lambda(a)=\Lambda(xa)
for all
x\in L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
a\in\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}
.

The upshot is that the pair
(L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\Delta)
with Haar weight
\varphi
is now a von Neumann algebraic quantum group
KK2003
. It can be easily verified that
\Lambda(\Phi^{\pm}(n,x,m))=\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}
, such that the GNS-map
\Lambda
indeed maps the basis elements of the essentially bounded operators to the basis elements of the square integrable functions. Moreover, it can be noted that
\Lambda
is invertible on the compactly supported functions. Physically, this map therefore gives a state-operator correspondence. The
*
-homomorphism
\pi
defines a left action of
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, given simply by
a.v=\pi(a)v
. Similarly, we can define a right action by
v.a=\pi(a^{*})v
for
a\in L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
v\in L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
with corresponding GNS-map
\Lambda^{*}(a)\coloneq\Lambda(a^{*})
. Note that the left and right actions are compatible with the inner product, i.e.
\langle v,a.w\rangle=\langle v.a,w\rangle
for
v,w\in L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.

Although the von Neumann algebraic setting gives a precise and formal framework to define the locally compact quantum group, some intuition is lost on the way: the coordinate functions we used to generate this von Neumann algebra were unbounded (as is common for many physically interesting operators), and thus are themselves not part of it. So, in order to get a bit more intuitive picture, let us extend the
*
-homomorphism and the map
\Lambda
in a natural way to also include the unbounded operators in
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. In particular, it follows from (
67
) that the (unbounded) action of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on the compactly supported functions in
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is given by

\begin{split}\alpha.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=\sqrt{\mathrm{sgn}(x)+|x|}~\delta_{n+1,q^{-2}x,m+1}^{\pm},\\
\gamma.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}~\delta_{n-1,x,m+1}^{\pm},\\
e.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=\mathrm{sgn}(x)\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}.\end{split}

(76)

Note here that
\left(e\gamma^{*}\gamma\right).\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}=x\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}
, such that its spectrum is given by
\sigma(e\gamma^{*}\gamma)=I_{q^{2}}\cup\{0\}
.

With the above action in mind, we define the set of formal infinite linear combinations of basis elements

\mathcal{F}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\coloneq\left\{\sum_{n,x,m,\varepsilon}f_{n,x,m}^{\varepsilon}\delta_{n,x,m}^{\varepsilon}:f_{n,x,m}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

(77)

which corresponds to functions over
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
which are not necessarily square-integrable.
44
44

44

One way to think about them is as distributions or generalised functions, dual to the compactly supported functions considered above. These can be given an appropriate topology (modelled on the usual case of distributions) to make the formally extended maps considered here continuous. We do not dwell on this issue further, since this is not the main focus here.
This space of functions splits into the direct sum
\mathcal{F}_{q}=\mathcal{F}^{+}_{q}\oplus\mathcal{F}^{-}_{q}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{+}
and
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{-}
are spanned by
\delta^{+}_{n,x,m}
and
\delta^{-}_{n,x,m}
, respectively. The GNS-map
\Lambda
can now be formally extended to the map

\tilde{\Lambda}\colon\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to\mathcal{F}_{q}^{+}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\quad\tilde{\Lambda}(a)=\sqrt{1-q^{2}}\sum_{x\in I_{q^{2}}}(a|\gamma|\otimes\mathrm{id}).\delta^{+}_{0,x,0}

(78)

and analogously we extend
\Lambda^{*}
to
\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}
. This trick allows us to turn the elements in
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\pm}
into actual functions over the locally compact quantum group. Namely, the coordinates
n,m
in a basis element
\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}
correspond to Fourier components, while the coordinate
x
corresponds simply to an evaluation at that point on the
q
-interval
I_{q^{2}}
. We therefore consider the basis elements as functions
\delta^{\pm}_{n,y,m}\colon[0,2\pi)\times I_{q^{2}}\times[-2\pi,2\pi)\to\mathbb{C}
given by

\delta^{\pm}_{n,y,m}(\theta,x,\psi)\coloneq e^{\frac{i}{2}(n\theta+m\psi)}\delta_{\pm yq^{n+m}}(x),

(79)

where
\delta_{y}
is the
q^{2}
-delta function as defined in
(
62
)
. Note here that the domains of
\theta
and
\psi
are different because of the additional constraint
n\equiv m\text{ mod }2
. Moreover, the parametrisation exactly corresponds to the Cartan decomposition of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
as given in (
14
). In this way, one can interpret the elements in
\mathcal{F}^{+}_{q}
as functions
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
, and we see that the ‘quantum group manifold’ is now parametrised by the (partially) discretised coordinates. That is, the operator-algebraic description allows us to describe the non-commutative group manifold as a discretised (but commutative) ‘manifold’ through the spectra of the non-commutative operators.

Similarly, through the GNS-map
\tilde{\Lambda}
, the elements in the coordinate algebra can be mapped to functions on the discretised coordinates. For example, for the generators
\alpha
and
\gamma
in
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, this gives

\begin{split}\tilde{\Lambda}(\alpha)(\theta,x,\psi)&=e^{i\frac{\theta+\psi}{2}}\sqrt{\mathrm{sgn}(x)+|x|},\\
\tilde{\Lambda}(\gamma)(\theta,x,\psi)&=e^{i\frac{\psi-\theta}{2}}\mathrm{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}.\end{split}

(80)

In the
q\to 1
limit and for
x=\sinh^{2}\rho/2
with
x\geq 0
, we recognise the same expressions as in the classical Cartan decomposition (
14
). For
x<-1
and
x=-\cosh^{2}\rho/2
, these correspond to functions over
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)v
. For arbitrary
f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{q}^{+}
, the inner product is given by

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\int_{-2\pi}^{2\pi}\frac{d\psi}{4\pi}\int_{I_{q^{2}}}d_{q^{2}}x~\overline{f(\theta,x,\psi)}g(\theta,x,\psi),

(81)

which, in the limit
q\to 1
, reduces to the Haar measure of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Indeed, for
x\geq 0
and
x=\sinh^{2}\rho/2
, this is exactly the Haar measure as given in (
15
).

Let us also comment on the functions in
\mathcal{F}^{-}_{q}
. On the level of the algebra, these functions correspond to the elements in
L_{q}^{\infty}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which contain a non-trivial factor of
u
. The functions in
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{-}
corresponding to
u\alpha
and
u\gamma
are given by

\begin{array}[]{ll}\tilde{\Lambda}(u\alpha)(\theta,x,\psi)&=e^{i\frac{\theta+\psi}{2}}\sqrt{\mathrm{sgn}(x)+|x|}\\
\tilde{\Lambda}(u\gamma)(\theta,x,\psi)&=e^{i\frac{\psi-\theta}{2}}\mathrm{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}\end{array}\qquad\text{for }|x|\geq 1

(82)

and they are zero for
|x|<1
. Note that these are the same functions as in (
80
), but with the additional constraint
|x|\geq 1
. Therefore, classically, one can think of
u\alpha
and
u\gamma
as functions over a restricted subspace of
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
. If we write the coordinates in the Gauss decomposition (
19
), this subspace corresponds to a restriction on the coordinate
\phi
, which, as we will see in Section
4.1
, gives rise to length positivity.

3.3
The regular representations

We can now also define the action of the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
on the square-integrable functions
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Similar to the coordinate algebra, these actions are given by unbounded operators and are therefore defined on the compactly supported functions. Inspired by
MMNSU19902
and
GKK2010
45
45

45

In particular, it can be seen that our left action is very similar to the one in Definition 4.1 in
GKK2010
, but adapted for our convention of basis elements. However, that paper didn’t consider the right action, and also the compatibility with the action of the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}
and the GNS map
\Lambda
was not explicitly discussed
, we define the left action to be given by

\begin{split}K.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=q^{\frac{m}{2}}\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm},\\
(q-q^{-1})E.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=-q^{\frac{m+1}{2}}\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m-2}x^{-1}}\delta_{n,x,m+2}^{\pm}+q^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}\sqrt{1+x^{-1}}\delta_{n,q^{-2}x,m+2}^{\pm},\\
(q-q^{-1})F.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=q^{\frac{m-1}{2}}\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m}x^{-1}}\delta_{n,x,m-2}^{\pm}-q^{-\frac{m-1}{2}}\sqrt{1+q^{-2}x^{-1}}\delta_{n,q^{2}x,m-2}^{\pm},\end{split}

(83)

and the right action to be given by

\begin{split}\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}.K&=q^{-\frac{n}{2}}\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm},\\
(q-q^{-1})\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}.E&=\mp q^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m-2}x^{-1}}e.\delta_{n+2,x,m}^{\pm}+q^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}\sqrt{1+x^{-1}}e.\delta_{n+2,q^{-2}x,m}^{\pm},\\
(q-q^{-1})\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}.F&=\pm q^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m}x^{-1}}e.\delta_{n-2,x,m}^{\pm}-q^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\sqrt{1+q^{-2}x^{-1}}e.\delta_{n-2,q^{2}x,m}^{\pm}.\end{split}

(84)

Note here that the Cartan element
K
has diagonal left and right actions on this basis, corresponding to the Cartan decomposition. These actions reduce to (
17
) in the limit
q\to 1
. Moreover, these actions have been constructed in such a way that they satisfy the following properties:

(a)

The left action and right action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
commute with each other;

(b)

The action of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on
L_{q}^{2}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is an equivariant representation with respect to the left and right action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
, that is
46
46

46

Here, we use Sweedler notation for the coproduct, that is
\Delta(X)=\sum X_{(1)}\otimes X_{(2)}
. Moreover, in the classical limit where the coproduct is given by
\Delta(X)=X\otimes 1+1\otimes X
, the relations reduce to
X\triangleright a=[X,a]
and
a\triangleleft X=[a,X]
, thus motivating these relations.

X.(a.f)=\sum(X_{(1)}\triangleright a).(X_{(2)}.f)\quad\text{and}\quad(f.a).X=\sum(f.X_{(1)}).(a\triangleleft X_{(2)});

(85)

(c)

X.\tilde{\Lambda}(a)=\tilde{\Lambda}(X\triangleright a)
and
\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}(a).X=\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}(a\triangleleft X)
;

for all
X\in\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
,
a\in\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
f\in L_{q}^{2}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
compactly supported. These properties can be verified by explicit calculation. The compatibility relations now allow us to define the left and right action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
on the compactly supported functions in
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
given by

X\triangleright a\coloneq(\Lambda)^{-1}(X.\Lambda(a)),\quad a\triangleleft X\coloneq(\Lambda^{*})^{-1}(\Lambda^{*}(a).X),

(86)

for
X\in\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
and
a\in L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
with
a
compactly supported. In summary, all the left and right actions are compatible with each other and with the GNS-map
\Lambda
, and we get the following picture:

L_{q}^{\infty}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
L_{q}^{2}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
\Lambda

(87)

In the previous section, we upgraded the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}
from a purely algebraic object to its von Neumann algebraic incarnation, and one can now do the same for the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}
. In particular, the von Neumann algebraic counterpart of this universal enveloping algebra is given by the (quantum)
Pontryagin dual
. The Pontryagin dual here denotes the full, non-abelian unitary dual of the operator algebraic quantum group
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, and
\hat{M}_{q}
should be thought of as the space of ‘essentially bounded functions’ on that unitary dual. This Pontryagin dual von Neumann algebraic quantum group can be readily described by using a special unitary operator, called
multiplicative unitary

W\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K})
, where
\mathcal{K}\coloneq L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
,
which in fact encodes most of the structure of the original von Neumann algebraic quantum group
M

CK2010
: for example, the coproduct on
L^{\infty}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is given by
\Delta(x)=W^{*}(1\otimes x)W
. The Pontryagin dual
47
47

47

For a locally compact abelian group
G
, it can be shown from here that
\hat{M}\cong L^{\infty}(\hat{G})
, with
\hat{G}
the dual group of irreducible unitary representations
CK2010
. This definition is therefore a far-reaching generalisation of the ordinary Pontryagin dual for locally compact abelian groups.
is then defined as the von Neumann algebra
\hat{M}_{q}\coloneq\overline{\{(\omega\otimes\iota)(W):\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K})_{*}\}}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K})
, with coproduct
\hat{\Delta}(\hat{x})=\Sigma W(\hat{x}\otimes 1)W^{*}\Sigma
, where
\Sigma
is the flip operator
\Sigma\colon x\otimes y\to y\otimes x
and
\hat{x}\in\hat{M}_{q}
. A remarkable fact is that
\hat{M}_{q}
is again a von Neumann algebraic quantum group
KV2003
. The intuition here is as just in classical Lie group case:
\hat{M}_{q}
should be thought of as an appropriate analogue of the group convolution algebra of (Hecke) integral operators acting on the functions on the group, whereas the subalgebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
should be thought of as representing ‘convolutions with the distributions supported at the quantum group unit element’.

Of course, the generators
E,F,K
of the universal enveloping algebra cannot themselves belong to
\hat{M}_{q}
(after all, as a von Neumann algebra, it can only contain bounded operators), but it was shown in
GKK2010
that these generators are affiliated with
\hat{M}_{q}
, which is ‘as close as an unbounded operator can get’ to belonging to / generating a von Neumann algebra. However, since the quantum group in consideration is the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, these operators are not enough to fully generate the dual (by affiliation construction). In particular, it is expected for the normaliser that the (symmetrised) Casimir operator
\tilde{\Omega}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}((q-q^{-1})^{2}\Omega+2)
does not commute with all elements of
\hat{M}_{q}
. Indeed, it can be shown
GKK2010
that there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading given by
\hat{M}_{q}=\hat{M}^{+}_{q}\oplus\hat{M}^{-}_{q}
such that
a\tilde{\Omega}\subseteq\tilde{\Omega}a
for
a\in\hat{M}_{q}^{+}
and
b\tilde{\Omega}\subseteq-\tilde{\Omega}b
for
b\in\hat{M}_{q}^{-}
. Therefore, there are additional generators
U_{0}^{+-},U_{0}^{-+}\in\hat{M}_{q}^{-}
for the Pontryagin dual that map eigenvectors of
\tilde{\Omega}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
to eigenvectors with eigenvalue
-\lambda

GKK2010
. By blending in these additional generators, one could replace
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
with
\hat{M}_{q}
in (
87
), but we won’t need it for the purposes of this paper.

Lastly, it was shown in
GKK2010
that the left regular representation decomposes into a direct integral of the principal unitary series, the discrete series and the strange series.
48
48

48

We refer to
BK1993
for the classification of irreducible representations of the quantum universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
.
The appearance of the principal unitary and the discrete series is, of course, expected from the regular representations of classical
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)

K2018
;
SL2R
. The appearance of the strange series can be thought of as an additional novel, inherently quantum, effect.

3.4
The quantum Gauss decomposition

In order to describe the reduction to the double-scaled SYK model, we need to derive the
quantum Gauss decomposition
of the (von Neumann algebraic) quantum group, in parallel to what was done in Section
2.2
for the classical case. We follow an approach similar to that of
K1993
;
KS2001
. Most calculations for this section are rather technical and are therefore deferred to Appendix
B
to maintain readability. Moreover, we will frequently use results for special functions as given in Appendix
A
.

To begin with, let us consider the quantum equivalent of the parabolic elements. We will look for them among the special class of elements given by

Y_{s}\coloneq q^{\frac{1}{2}}E-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}F-(s+s^{-1})\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}

(88)

for
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. These are the so-called
twisted primitive elements
, whose coproduct reads
\Delta(Y_{s})=K\otimes Y_{s}+Y_{s}\otimes K^{-1}
and thus closely resembles the coproduct of a classical Lie algebra element. It can be seen that, with appropriate scaling of parameters, these reduce to the parabolic elements (
18
) in the classical limit, namely
\lim_{q\to 1}Y_{q^{n}}=2i\tilde{E}
and
\lim_{q\to 1}Y_{-q^{m}}=2i\tilde{F}
. Moreover, in the limit
s\to\infty
, we define

Y_{\infty}\coloneq\lim_{s\to\infty}s^{-1}(q-q^{-1})Y_{s}=K^{-1}-K,

(89)

which, in the classical limit, corresponds to the classical Cartan element. Unlike the classical case, however, these twisted primitive elements are not self-adjoint. Therefore, we instead consider the slightly modified elements
Y_{s}K
and
K^{-1}Y_{s}
, which are self-adjoint. For future convenience, we also introduce the symmetrised (in the sense of having a symmetric spectrum around
0
) twisted primitive elements, given by

\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\left[(q-q^{-1})K^{-1}Y_{t}+(t+t^{-1})\right],\quad\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\left[(q-q^{-1})Y_{s}K-(s+s^{-1})\right].

(90)

The left and right actions of these versions of twisted primitive elements will correspond to symmetric (i.e. formally Hermitian) operators in the left and right regular representations.

The quantum Gauss decomposition
49
49

49

Throughout the paper, we will occasionally refer to the decomposition with general values of
s,t
as the ‘quantum Gauss decomposition’.
now corresponds to functions that have diagonal left action of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
and diagonal right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
for
t=q^{n}
and
s=-q^{m}
. Note here that, unlike the corresponding parabolic elements in the classical case, the variables
s
and
t
can take on any power of
q
. In fact, as was shown in
S2003
, diagonalisation with respect to the twisted primitive elements gives the quantum group a dynamical structure, and the variables
s
and
t
are the corresponding dynamical parameters. In
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, we define the elements

\alpha_{st}=\alpha+qt^{-1}e\gamma^{*}-s^{-1}\gamma-s^{-1}t^{-1}e\alpha^{*},\quad\gamma_{st}=-s^{-1}\alpha-qs^{-1}t^{-1}e\gamma^{*}+\gamma+t^{-1}e\alpha^{*},

(91)

on which
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
acts diagonally from the left and
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
diagonally acts from the right
K1993
;
KS2001
.
50
50

50

We use a different convention than
KS2001
. In particular, we consider slightly different elements when looking at the left and right action of the twisted primitive elements, and use a different normalisation such that the limit
s,t\to\infty
of these elements is well-defined.
Here, it can be seen that
\alpha_{\infty,\infty}=\alpha
and
\gamma_{\infty,\infty}=\gamma
, which is also expected as the limit
s,t\to\infty
corresponds to the Cartan decomposition. Moreover, these elements satisfy dynamical commutation relations
KS2001
. For example, we have
\alpha_{q^{-1}s,qt}\gamma_{st}=q\gamma_{qs,qt}\alpha_{st}
. The dynamical structure reveals itself in the way the parameters change values upon multiplication of the corresponding algebra elements.

Next, we can consider the generalisation of the spherical element
e\gamma^{*}\gamma
, i.e. the position operator, for the Gauss decomposition. That is, consider an element on which
Y_{t}
and
Y_{s}
act trivially from the left and the right, respectively. We define
KS2001
:
51
51

51

We remark here that the spherical element in
KS2001
was defined as an element in
\mathrm{SL}_{q}(2,\mathbb{C})
. Thus, the corresponding spherical element of the normaliser is obtained by imposing the
*
-structure of the latter, as opposed to the one of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
.

\begin{split}\rho_{st}\coloneq e\gamma^{*}_{s,qt}\gamma_{s,qt}-s^{-2}-q^{-2}t^{-2}.\end{split}

(92)

Here, it can be noted that
\rho_{\infty,\infty}=e\gamma^{*}\gamma
, i.e. it reduces to the spherical element for the Cartan decomposition in the limit
s,t\to\infty
. Moreover, it is an
(s,t)
-spherical element
, i.e.
Y_{t}\triangleright\rho_{st}=0=\rho_{st}\triangleleft Y_{s}
. In particular, by a straightforward adaptation of the argument from
KS2001
(see footnote
51
) one sees that
\rho_{st}
(together with the trivial spherical element
e
) generates the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
of
(s,t)
-spherical elements, i.e.

\mathcal{A}_{q}((Y_{s})\backslash\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}/(Y_{t}))=\langle\rho_{st},e\rangle,

(93)

where
(Y_{s})
denotes the Lie group generated by the classical limit of
Y_{s}
. The elements
\alpha_{st}
,
\gamma_{st}
and
\rho_{st}
satisfy the relations

\alpha_{s,qt}\rho_{st}=q^{2}\rho_{qs,qt}\alpha_{s,qt}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{s,qt}\rho_{st}=\rho_{q^{-1}s,qt}\gamma_{s,qt}.

(94)

Classically, i.e. in the
q\to 1
limit, it can be calculated that
\rho_{-1,1}(g(\gamma_{L},\phi,\gamma_{R}))=-2+4e^{2\phi}
in the Gauss decomposition (
19
). Therefore, the spherical element
\rho_{st}
corresponds to the coordinate
\phi
, and in the quantum case, a deformation of this coordinate. Moreover, the eigenvalues of the left and right action of
Y_{t}K
and
K^{-1}Y_{s}
will correspond to the quantised momenta
p_{L}
and
p_{R}
.

Thus, we are interested in finding a basis of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
which diagonalises the left action of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
, the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
and the left/right action of
\rho_{st}
. This diagonalisation has to be done in several steps. As a start, we will derive the
quantum Iwasawa decomposition
, in which we diagonalise the basis first with respect to
\rho_{\infty,t}
and then with respect to the left action of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
. Afterwards, we will diagonalise with respect to
\rho_{st}
and the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
to obtain the quantum Gauss decomposition. This derivation will therefore take four different diagonalisation steps.

Using the left and right action of the universal enveloping algebra in (
83
) and (
84
), the actions of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
,
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
On the basis
\{\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}\}
, as given in (
73
), can be calculated to be

\begin{split}\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}&=A^{\pm}(n,x,m)\delta_{n,x,m+2}^{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}q^{-m}x^{-1}t\rho_{\infty,t}.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}+A^{\pm}(n,x,m-2)\delta_{n,x,m-2}^{\pm},\\
\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}.\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}&=\pm A^{\pm}(n,x,m)e.\delta_{n+2,x,m}^{\pm}-\frac{1}{2}q^{-n}x^{-1}s\rho_{s,\infty}.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}\pm A^{\pm}(n-2,x,m)e.\delta_{n-2,x,m}^{\pm},\end{split}

(95)

where
A^{\pm}(n,x,m)=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m-2}x^{-1}}
. In order to diagonalise with respect to, for example, the left action of

, we would like to bring the form of the action into that of a three-term recurrence relation, such that we can use the results on special functions as described in Appendix
A
.

Therefore, let us begin with the diagonalisation of
\rho_{\infty,t}
. From this point onwards, we will always assume

t\rho_{\infty,t}=\gamma\alpha+\alpha^{*}\gamma^{*}+(t+t^{-1})e\gamma^{*}\gamma

\left(t\rho_{\infty,t}\right).\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}=q^{-1}x\sqrt{1+x^{-1}}\delta_{n,q^{-2}x,m+2}^{\pm}+(t+t^{-1})x\delta_{n,x,m}^{\pm}+qx\sqrt{1+q^{-2}x^{-1}}\delta_{n,q^{2}x,m-2}^{\pm}.

(96)

For

\end{split}

(97)

where
p_{k}^{t^{-2}}

with
\rho_{\infty,t}.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}=xg^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
for

, where the spectrum of (an appropriate self-adjoint extension

A.3
, respectively. Note that these
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
form a (generalised) orthonormal basis of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, that is

\langle\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)},\delta^{\varepsilon^{\prime},(\infty,t)}_{n^{\prime},x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu}(\mu(\xi)-\mu(\xi^{\prime})),

(102)

where
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\delta_{\nu}
is the delta-function with respect to the measure
\nu
as defined in (
9
), which is just the Dirac-delta function for
\mu(\xi),\mu(\xi^{\prime})
in the continuous spectrum and the Kronecker delta function in the discrete spectrum.

We can now see that these basis elements correspond to the Iwasawa decomposition of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, that is

\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}.\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}=\mu(\xi)\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)},\quad\rho_{\infty,t}.\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}=x\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)},\quad\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}.K=q^{-\frac{n}{2}}\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}.

(103)

From (
100
) it follows that the total spectrum of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
on
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is given by

\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L})=\{-\mu(tq^{m}):|tq^{m}|<1\ \mathrm{for}\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup[-1,1]\cup\{\mu(t^{-1}q^{m}):|t^{-1}q^{m}|<1\ \mathrm{for}\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

(104)

However, not all
\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}
are non-zero for general
n,x,\xi
, as the conditions in (
100
) need to be satisfied.

The action of the coordinate algebra on the basis in the Iwasawa decomposition has been calculated in Appendix
B.1
and is given by

\begin{split}\alpha_{\infty,qt}.\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}&=-e(x)\sqrt{e(x)(1+x)}~\delta_{n+1,q^{-2}x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,qt)},\\
\gamma_{\infty,qt}.\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}&=e(x)\sqrt{e(x)(q^{-2}t^{-2}+x)}~\delta_{n-1,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,qt)},\end{split}

(105)

The dynamical structure of the quantum group here can be seen from the fact that the value of
t
changes when acting on basis elements with the coordinate algebra.
Note that if we take
\xi=t^{-1}q^{m}
, then the vectors
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,tq^{m}}
reduce to
\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}
in the limit
t\to\infty
. Similarly, the actions of the coordinate algebra reduce to those of the Cartan decomposition (
76
) up to a phase difference.

Next, we would like to diagonalise this basis with respect to the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
. As calculated in Appendix
B.1
, the right action of the twisted primitive element is given by

\begin{gathered}\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}.\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}=A(n,x,\xi)\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n+2,x,\xi}-q^{-n-1}(tx)^{-1}s\tilde{\rho}_{st}.\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}+A(n-2,x,\xi)\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n-2,x,\xi},\\
A(n,x,\xi)=\mp\mathrm{sgn}(x)\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1\pm\xi^{-1}(tx)^{-1}q^{-2-n})(1\pm\xi(tx)^{-1}q^{-2-n})},\end{gathered}

(106)

where
54
54

54

We introduce this renormalised version of the spherical elements, since its continuous spectrum is simply given by the interval
[-1,1]
. Note, however, that
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
does not have well-defined limits
s,t\to\infty
. Instead, for example,
\lim_{s\to\infty}s^{-1}\tilde{\rho}_{st}=-\frac{1}{2}qt\rho_{\infty,t}
.

\tilde{\rho}_{st}\coloneq-\frac{1}{2}qst\rho_{st}=\frac{1}{2}qte\gamma_{\infty,qt}^{*}\alpha_{\infty,qt}-\frac{1}{2}qt(s+s^{-1})\rho_{\infty,t}+\frac{1}{2}qte\alpha_{\infty,qt}^{*}\gamma_{\infty,qt}.

(107)

Therefore, we should again first diagonalise with respect to the spherical element
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
to make
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
into a three-term recurrence relation. Using (
105
), the action of
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
can be calculated to be given by

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\rho}_{st}.\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}=A(x)\delta_{n+2,q^{-2}x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}+B(x)\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}+A(q^{2}x)\delta_{n-2,q^{2}x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)},\\
A(x)=-\frac{1}{2}e(x)\sqrt{(1+x)(1+t^{2}x)},\quad B(x)=-\frac{1}{2}qt(s+s^{-1})x.\end{gathered}

(108)

One can note that
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
acts irreducibly on the three distinct subspaces
55
55

55

Note here that an additional irreducible subspace
V
appears in the Iwasawa decomposition, which has to do with the richer spectrum of
\sigma(\rho_{\infty,t})
. This subspace vanishes in the limit
t\to\infty
.

\begin{split}U&=\overline{\text{span}}\{\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}:x=-q^{-2k-2}\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},\\
V&=\overline{\text{span}}\{\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}:x=-t^{-2}q^{2k}\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},\\
W&=\overline{\text{span}}\{\delta_{n,x,\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)}:x=q^{-2k}\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{Z}\},\end{split}

(109)

where
\overline{\text{span}}
denotes a closed linear span. For

, one can recognize that the action of the spherical element

-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (

). Similarly, for

q^{2}
-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials. Lastly, for

5
). Therefore, it follows from Propositions

and

that the spectrum of (an appropriate self-adjoint extension

\begin{split}\sigma\left(\tilde{\rho}_{st}|_{U}\right)&=\left\{\mu(qs^{-1}t^{-1}q^{2k}):k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\},\\
\sigma\left(\tilde{\rho}_{st}|_{V}\right)&=[-1,1]\cup\left\{\mu(q^{-1}s^{-1}tq^{-2k}):|q^{-1}s^{-1}tq^{-2k}|<1\ \mathrm{for}\ k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\},\\
\sigma\left(\tilde{\rho}_{st}|_{W}\right)&=[-1,1]\cup\left\{\mu(q^{-1}st^{-1}q^{-2k}):|q^{-1}st^{-1}q^{-2k}|<1\ \mathrm{for}\ k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\}\\
&\qquad\qquad\cup\left\{-\mu(q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}q^{2k}):|q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}q^{2k}|<1\ \mathrm{for}\ k\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.\end{split}

The corresponding eigenvectors are given by

\begin{split}h_{n,y_{U},\xi}^{\pm,(st)}&\coloneq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}h_{k}(\mu(y_{U});q^{-1}st,q^{-1}s^{-1}t;q^{-2})\delta_{n+2k+2,-q^{-2k-2},\xi}^{\pm,(\infty,t)},\\

for

Let us introduce the ‘indicator’ functions for the irreducible subspaces described above. Namely, define
\kappa\colon\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})\to\{\pm 1\}
, where

for

\displaystyle h^{-e(y),(st)}_{n,y,\varepsilon t^{-\varepsilon}q^{m}}\text{ non-zero}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\kappa(y)=\varepsilon\text{ and }n\leq-m-2

(112)

\displaystyle h^{e(y),(st)}_{n,y,\varepsilon t^{-\varepsilon}q^{m}}\text{ non-zero}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\bigg[\kappa(y)=-\varepsilon\text{ and }n\geq m\bigg]\text{ or }\kappa(y)=\varepsilon

.

Note that if

We are now ready to diagonalise with respect to the right action of

\begin{gathered}h_{n,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}.\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}=A(n,y,\xi)h_{n+2,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}+e(y)\kappa(y)t^{-\kappa(y)}\mu(y)q^{-n-1}h_{n,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}+A(n-2,y,\xi)h_{n-2,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)},\\
A(n,y,\xi)=\mp e(y)\kappa(y)\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1\pm e(y)\kappa(y)\xi^{-1}t^{-\kappa(y)}q^{-2-n})(1\pm e(y)\kappa(y)\xi t^{-\kappa(y)}q^{-2-n})}.\end{gathered}

(113)

In order to ‘diagnose’ this difference operator, we have to go through the different cases in which

Note here that there is a multiplicity in the continuous spectra of the operators. In particular, for basis elements

The action of the coordinate algebra on the basis in the Gauss decomposition is calculated in Appendix
B.2
, and is given by

\begin{split}\alpha_{s,qt}.\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}&=\sqrt{e(y)(1+q^{-2}s^{-2}t^{-2}-2q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}\mu(y))}~\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\pm,(qs,qt)},\\
\gamma_{s,qt}.\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}&=e(y)\sqrt{e(y)(s^{-2}+q^{-2}t^{-2}-2q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}\mu(y))}~\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\pm,(q^{-1}s,qt)}.\end{split}

(124)

The dynamical structure of the quantum group in this decomposition with respect to twisted primitive elements becomes apparent in the change of both the dynamical parameters
s
and
t
here. Taking
x=-2q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}\mu(y)
(to undo the normalisation on
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
(

)) and
\eta=-s^{-1}q^{n}
, the basis elements and the above action reduce to the Iwasawa decomposition in the limit

. If we specialise to the quantum Gauss decomposition, i.e. set

and

with

, the spectra of
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
,
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
and
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
are given by

\begin{gathered}\sigma\left(\tilde{\rho}_{st}\right)=-\cosh\left(\left(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\ln q^{2}\right)\cup[-1,1]\cup\cosh\left(\left(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\ln q^{2}\right),\\
\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L})=-\cosh\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\ln q^{2}\right)\cup[-1,1]\cup\cosh\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\ln q^{2}\right)=\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}),\end{gathered}

(125)

with the additional constraints as given by (
114
), (

) and (
119
).

Let us summarise these constraints for the discrete spectra. Let
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})\setminus[-1,1]
, then the basis elements
\delta^{\pm,(st)}_{\xi,y,\eta}
are non-zero in the following cases:

•

For
\xi=\kappa(y)t^{-\kappa(y)}q^{m}
and
\mu(\eta)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})\setminus[-1,1]
such that
|\xi|,|\eta|<1
,

\displaystyle\delta^{-e(y),(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}

\displaystyle\text{ non-zero}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=e(y)q\eta\xi q^{2k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{for }

\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

(126)

\displaystyle\delta^{e(y),(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}

\displaystyle\text{ non-zero}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=-e(y)q^{-1}\eta^{-1}\xi q^{-2k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{for }

\displaystyle 0\leq k<\log_{q^{2}}|\xi|-\log_{q^{2}}|\eta|

\displaystyle\text{or}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=e(y)q\eta\xi q^{2k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{for }

\displaystyle k\geq-(\log_{q^{2}}|\xi|+\log_{q^{2}}|\eta|).

•

for
\xi=-\kappa(y)t^{\kappa(y)}q^{m}
and
\mu(\eta)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})\setminus[-1,1]
such that
|\xi|,|\eta|<1
,

\begin{split}&\delta^{-e(y),(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}=0\\
&\delta^{e(y),(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}\text{ non-zero}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad y=e(y)q^{-1}\eta\xi^{-1}q^{-2k}\quad\text{for }0\leq k<\log_{q^{2}}|\eta|-\log_{q^{2}}|\xi|.\end{split}

(127)

Note that these constraints are only there for the discrete spectra. If one would pick
\mu(y)\in[-1,1]
and
\mu(\xi)\notin[-1,1]
, then it follows from (
116
) that
\mu(\eta)
can take any value in
\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})
for
\delta^{e(y),(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
to be non-zero. Similarly, if
\mu(\xi)\in[-1,1]
and
\mu(y)\not\in[-1,1]
, then it follows that
\mu(\eta)\in[-1,1]
, or
\eta
is discrete with
\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)=e(y)\kappa(y)\mathrm{sgn}(ty)
for
\delta^{\pm,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
to be non-zero. Lastly, if
\mu(\xi),\mu(y)\in[-1,1]
, then it is enforced that also
\mu(\eta)\in[-1,1]
.

3
, we give a plot of the allowed spectra
