for the subset of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
spanned by
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
with
(s,t)=(-1,1)
and
\xi=q
. The continuous spectra have a possible multiplicity. Moreover, the discrete points can be divided into four quadrants based on the signs of
\varepsilon
and
e(y)
. Note here that only the top-right quadrant is present in the representation theory of pure (formal)
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
, and the other three quadrants appear due to the extension to the normaliser. We remark that only a bounded region of the spectra is shown: the spectra extend infinitely far in all directions.

Figure 3:
Plot of the allowed spectra
\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})
and
\varepsilon\cdot\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})
for the subspace of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
spanned by
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
with
(s,t)=(-1,1)
and
\xi=q
, for
q=0.95
. The continuous spectra have a possible multiplicity. Moreover, the discrete spectra can be divided into four quadrants based on the value of
(\varepsilon,e(y))
. In particular, the top-left corresponds to
(-1,1)
, the top-right to
(1,1)
, the bottom-left to
(-1,-1)
and the bottom-right to
(1,-1)
. The top-left quadrant, which is coloured in grey, corresponds to the
chord subsector
, as defined in Section
4
.

Let us now also make the connection to the classical coordinates of the Gauss decomposition as in Section
2.2
. There we saw that the eigenvalues of
\tilde{E}
and
\tilde{F}
correspond to the momenta
p_{R}
and
p_{L}
, respectively. As we have
\lim_{q\to 1}K^{-1}Y_{q^{n}}=2i\tilde{E}
and since
K^{-1}Y_{t}
is self-adjoint, we infer that the quantised coordinate
p_{R}
should correspond to the eigenvalues of the left action of
\frac{1}{2}K^{-1}Y_{t}
. Similarly, the quantised coordinate
p_{L}
corresponds to the eigenvalues of the right action of
\frac{1}{2}Y_{s}K
. Lastly, in the limit
q\to 1
,
\tilde{\rho}_{-1,1}
corresponds to the classical coordinate
-1+2e^{2\phi}
, that is,
\tilde{\rho}_{-1,1}(g(\gamma_{L},\phi,\gamma_{R}))=-1+2e^{2\phi}
. This, therefore, gives the following correspondence between the quantised variables and the classical variables in the Gauss decomposition:

p_{L}=-\varepsilon\frac{\mu(\eta)+\mu(s)}{q^{-1}-q},\quad e^{\phi}=\mu(y^{1/2}),\quad p_{R}=\frac{\mu(\xi)-\mu(t)}{q^{-1}-q},

(128)

where
\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2}(x+x^{-1})
,
\mu(\eta)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})
,
\mu(\xi)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{t}^{R})
and
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})
with
\text{Re}(y)>-1
. Note here that we need
\text{Re}(y)>-1
for
\mu(y^{1/2})
to be positive, which corresponds to the restriction to
\mathrm{SU}_{q}^{\circ}(1,1)
as in Section
2.2
. For
\text{Re}(y)<-1
, we go outside of the subset of Gauss decomposable elements, and there is no relation to
\phi
. Moreover, we have an additional factor of
-\varepsilon
in the definition of
p_{L}
, such that
p_{L}p_{R}>0
will correspond to elements in
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{-}
. As we will see in Section
4
, this is indeed the correct choice to get the correct classical reduction in the
q\to 1
limit. The von Neumann algebraic quantum group description now shows explicitly how these coordinates become quantised through the discrete spectra of the associated operators.

3.5
The Casimir operator

We can now calculate the action of the Casimir element of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
on the different basis elements in the decompositions that we studied above. Here, we will consider a more symmetric version of the Casimir element, given by
\tilde{\Omega}=\frac{1}{2}(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Omega+1
, with
\Omega
the Casimir element as defined in (
50
). By the definition of the left action of the universal enveloping algebra on
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
in (
83
), the action of the Casimir operator in the Cartan decomposition can be directly calculated as:
60
60

60

From now on, we will denote the superscript of the basis elements with
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
instead of
\pm
, to allow for nicer notation in (
130
).

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Omega}.\delta_{n,x,m}^{\varepsilon}=A(x)\delta_{n,q^{-2}x,m}^{\varepsilon}+B(x)\delta_{n,x,m}^{\varepsilon}+A(q^{2}x)\delta_{n,q^{2}x,m}^{\varepsilon},\\
A(x)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1+x^{-1})(1+\varepsilon q^{-n-m}x^{-1})},\quad B(x)=-\frac{1}{2}(q^{-m}+\varepsilon q^{-n})q^{-1}x^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(129)

The action of the Casimir operator in the Gauss decomposition can be calculated through the intermediate bases given in the previous section. This is a rather technical derivation, which can be found in our Appendix
B.1
and
B.2
. For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})\setminus[-1,1]
, we derived the action of the Casimir operator in the Gauss decomposition to be given by the following
q
-difference operator:

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Omega}.\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}=A(y)\delta_{\eta,q^{-2}y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}+B(y)\delta_{\eta,y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}+A(y^{-1})\delta_{\eta,q^{2}y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)},\\
A(y)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{(1-q^{-1}s^{\pm}t^{\pm}y)(1+q^{-1}(\varepsilon\eta)^{\pm}\xi^{\pm}y)}{(1-y^{2})(1-q^{-2}y^{2})^{2}(1-q^{-4}y^{2})}},\\
B(y)=\frac{\left[\mu(s)\mu(y)-\mu(q)\mu(t)\right]\mu(\xi)-\left[\mu(t)\mu(y)-\mu(q)\mu(s)\right]\mu(\varepsilon\eta)}{(\mu(y)+\mu(q))(\mu(y)-\mu(q))},\end{gathered}

(130)

where

(1-q^{-1}s^{\pm}t^{\pm}y)\coloneq(1-q^{-1}sty)(1-q^{-1}s^{-1}ty)(1-q^{-1}st^{-1}y)(1-q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}y).

(131)

Note here that this action can, in principle, be naturally extended to all
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})
. However, one has to be careful with the fact that
\mu(q^{2}y)
and
\mu(q^{-2}y)
are not always in the spectrum of
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
, in which case the resulting vector on the right hand side becomes a more complicated linear combination of eigenvectors. Of course, this does not mean that the Casimir action is not defined on the whole spectrum, but just that for the boundary spectral points, one cannot uniformly write down this action as a three-term recurrence relation in terms of our orthonormal basis. If we take
x=-2q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}\mu(y)
and
\eta=-s^{-1}q^{n}
, then the action (
130
) reduces to the action in the Iwasawa decomposition (
16
) in the limit
s\to\infty
. If we then also take
\xi=t^{-1}q^{m}
, then the action reduces to the action in the Cartan decomposition (
129
) in the limit
t\to\infty
.

The Casimir operator action (
130
) corresponds to the recurrence relation for the Askey-Wilson functions/polynomials. To see this, consider the rescaled (non-normalised) eigenbasis given by the elements

\tilde{\delta}^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}=\sqrt{\frac{(qsty,qsty^{-1},-q\varepsilon\eta\xi y,-q\varepsilon\eta\xi y^{-1};q^{2})_{\infty}}{|y^{-1}-y|(qs^{-1}ty,qs^{-1}ty^{-1},-q(\varepsilon\eta)^{-1}\xi y,-q(\varepsilon\eta)^{-1}\xi y^{-1};q^{2})_{\infty}}}\delta^{\pm,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi},

(132)

and define the shift operator
\mathcal{T}_{q^{2}}.\tilde{\delta}^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}=\tilde{\delta}^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,q^{2}y,\xi}
. The action of the (non-symmetric) Casimir element
\Omega
on
\tilde{\delta}^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
can then be calculated to yield:

-qs\varepsilon\eta(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Omega=\psi(y)(\mathcal{T}_{q^{2}}-1)+\psi(y^{-1})(\mathcal{T}_{q^{-2}}-1)+(1+qs\varepsilon\eta)^{2}

(133)

where

\psi(y)\coloneq\frac{(1-qst^{-1}y)(1-qsty)(1+q\varepsilon\eta\xi^{-1}y)(1+q\varepsilon\eta\xi y)}{(1-y^{2})(1-q^{2}y^{2})}.

(134)

Here, one can recognise the
q
-difference equation for the Askey-Wilson functions and polynomials as given in
KS2000
;
KS2001_3
. Moreover, this expression is well-defined for all
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})
over the space of functions on the support of the measure corresponding to the Askey-Wilson functions. This action reduces to the action given by Koelink and Stokman in
KS2001
for
\varepsilon=+1
,
\eta=-s^{-1}q^{2i}
and
\xi=t^{-1}q^{2j}
. Koelink and Stokman only considered this action in the representation theory of the algebraic quantum group
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
, as by this time the locally compact quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
had not yet been constructed. Therefore, the above action is a generalisation of the Gauss decomposition to the locally compact (dynamical) quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

4
Reduction to the double-scaled SYK model

In the previous section, we have described all the necessary ingredients to consider a particle-on-a-quantum-group-manifold theory that corresponds to the double-scaled SYK model. Namely, as discussed in
BINT2019
;
BINN2023
, the bulk theory corresponding to the DSSYK model is realised on a quantum deformation of AdS
2
space, which in turn can be realised as a
q
-homogeneous space of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Similar to Section
2.3
, the quantum homogeneous spaces relevant for the double-scaled SYK model can be constructed by restricting to subspaces with fixed left and/or right actions of the parabolic elements on the function space, the eigenvalues of which impose constraints on the homogeneous spaces, and are therefore additional parameters in the reduced theory. In particular, consider the
quantum Gauss decomposition
, i.e.
(s,t)\in\left(-q^{-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\right)\times q^{-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
such that
|s|,|t|>q
, and restrict to the subsector of vectors given by
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
with
\varepsilon=-1
,
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\rho_{st}|_{W})\setminus[-1,1]
with
0<y<1
,
\xi=t^{-1}q^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
0<\xi<1
, and
\mu(\eta)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})^{-}_{y,\xi}
such that
0<\eta<1
. Since
\kappa(y)=+1
,
\varepsilon=-1
and
e(y)=+1
, it follows from the (
126
) that

y=q\eta\xi q^{2k},\quad\eta,\xi<1\quad\text{and}\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(135)

such that in particular
y\leq q\eta\xi
. We will call the subspace of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
spanned by these vectors the
chord subsector
. This subspace corresponds to the top-left quadrant
61
61

61

Note that we could have instead chosen the chord subsector to correspond to the bottom-right quadrant in Figure
3
, which would have given the same result. In that case, the states do not correspond to the ‘Gauss decomposable subset’
\mathrm{SU}_{q}^{\circ}(1,1)
, but rather to
\left(-\mathrm{SU}_{q}^{\circ}(1,1)\right)
.
of Figure
3
.

It might be unexpected that the chord subsector lies in
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{-}
, i.e. corresponds to
\varepsilon=-1
. However, we do argue that this is indeed the correct subsector to consider. Namely, the eigenstates of the transfer matrix in the double-scaled SYK model are given by the continuous
q
-Hermite polynomials
BNS2018
;
BINT2019
. One does not expect these polynomials to appear in the representation theory of purely (formal)
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
, as the eigenfunctions of the Casimir operator in its regular representation correspond to the Askey-Wilson functions
KS2001
. However, in the extension to the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the action of the Casimir operator (
130
) restricted to the chord subsector is instead precisely given by the recurrence relation for the Askey-Wilson polynomials
62
62

62

Note here the important distinction between the Askey-Wilson
functions
and the Askey-Wilson
polynomials
. In particular, the Askey-Wilson functions diagonalise the corresponding doubly-infinite Jacobi operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, while the Askey-Wilson polynomials diagonalise a Jacobi operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
. It should be noted that for specific values of the spectral parameters, the Askey-Wilson functions may reduce to the Askey-Wilson polynomials
KS2001_3
, but that such a reduction does not appear in the allowed spectra for
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)

KS2001
.

AW1985
(see also
KLS2010
) with
a=qs\eta,b=qt^{-1}\xi,c=qt\xi
and
d=qs^{-1}\eta
. The continuous
q
-Hermite polynomials, and hence the transfer matrix for the double-scaled SYK model, can then be obtained from the Askey-Wilson polynomials by taking the limit
a,b,c,d\to 0

KLS2010
. Therefore, we consider the rescaling of variables

\eta\to q^{r}\eta,\quad\xi\to q^{r}\xi,\quad y\to q^{2r}y,

(136)

and, upon (
128
), also the rescaling of the classical variables

p_{L}\to q^{-r}p_{L},\quad p_{R}\to q^{-r}p_{R},\quad\phi\to\phi-r\ln q,

(137)

and take the limit
r\to\infty
. Note that this rescaling is compatible with the chosen coordinates (
135
), and can physically be interpreted as zooming into the boundary of quantum AdS
2
. Moreover, the classical Casimir action (
27
) is invariant under such a rescaling, such that classically the above transformation is just a symmetry.

Figure 4:
Plot of the allowed rescaled coordinates
y^{-1}
and
\eta^{-1}
for the subspace of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
spanned by the rescaled states
\lim_{r\to\infty}\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{q^{r}\eta,q^{2r}y,q^{r}\xi}
with
(s,t)=(-1,1)
and
\xi=q
, for
q=0.95
. Note that this plot now only contains discrete points. The chord subsector is coloured in grey.

In Figure
4
, we plotted the allowed rescaled coordinates
y^{-1}
and
\eta^{-1}
for the subspace of
L^{2}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
spanned by the rescaled
\lim_{r\to\infty}\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{q^{r}\eta,q^{2r}y,q^{r}\xi}
with
(s,t)=(-1,1)
and
\xi=q
. Note that this plot is obtained by zooming ‘infinitely far’ out of the plot in Figure
3
, such that the continuous spectra decouple, and the discrete points can now get arbitrarily close to the two axes. Also here, only a bounded region of the coordinates is shown, and they extend infinitely far in all directions.

In these rescaled coordinates, the relation to the classical coordinates (
128
) becomes

p_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\eta^{-1}}{q^{-1}-q},\quad e^{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}y^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad p_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\xi^{-1}}{q^{-1}-q},

(138)

where
\eta,\xi\in q^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\log_{q}\eta\equiv\log_{q}\xi\text{ mod }2
and
y\in q^{2\mathbb{Z}+1}
such that
y\leq q\eta\xi
and
\varepsilon=-1
. Note here that
p_{L}p_{R}>0
. It follows from (
135
) that the value of
y
is determined by the non-negative integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Therefore, for
\eta,\xi\in q^{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\log_{q}\eta\equiv\log_{q}\xi\text{ mod }2
, we denote the vectors in the chord subsector in these rescaled coordinates by

|k\rangle_{\eta,\xi}\coloneq\lim_{r\to\infty}\delta^{-,(st)}_{q^{r}\eta,q^{2r}y,q^{r}\xi}\quad\text{with }y=q\eta\xi q^{2k}\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(139)

which we will call the
chord states
.

4.1
Length positivity

Similarly to Section
2.4
, the chord states
|k\rangle_{\eta,\xi}
correspond to wormhole states with boundary conditions
\eta
and
\xi
, but now separated by
k
chords. These states have the natural interpretation of counting the chord number in the two-sided picture of the double-scaled SYK model
L2022
. Pictorially, a chord state is given by

|k\rangle_{\eta,\xi}=
\eta
\xi
k

(140)

where the geodesic length
L
in (
30
) has been replaced by the chord number
k
. Remember from Section
2.4
that the coordinate
\phi
corresponds to the renormalised geodesic length, i.e.
2\phi=2L+\ln\varepsilon
, and we had to impose length positivity
L\geq 0
by hand. Here, equation (
138
) gives the relation between the quantised coordinate
\phi
and the non-negative chord number
k
(via (
135
)). It can now be noted that the constraint
y\leq q\eta\xi
also gives a constraint on
\phi
. In particular, in terms of the classical coordinates, we have

2\phi\geq\ln\left(p_{L}p_{R}\lambda^{2}\right)\quad\text{for }\lambda\to 0,

(141)

where
q=e^{-\lambda/2}
and
\lambda
is the renormalization parameter. If one imposes the asymptotic
AdS
2
boundary conditions
p_{L},p_{R}\sim\lambda^{-1}
, the coordinate
\phi
is forced to be positive, leading to length positivity of the bare geodesic length. This gives a clean, first principle derivation of the length positivity, which we regard as an improvement over
BMT2025
, where the derivation of the gravitational matrix elements was based on a representation of a so-called
twisted Hopf
*
-algebra
, corresponding to
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}))
with unusual unitarity properties (see also our discussion in Section
4.5
). Notice that in other earlier discussions (see e.g.
BM2024
;
BINN2023
), length positivity had to be imposed by hand. Here, length positivity is a consequence of the constraint on the allowed eigenvalues for
\eta
,
\xi
and
y
in the von Neumann algebraic description of the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Note that the normaliser plays a significant role in the length positivity, as the chord subsector corresponds to states with
\varepsilon=-1
.

4.2
The partition function and
n
-point functions

We can now repeat the considerations of Section
2.4
for the
q
-deformed case. While the resulting expressions are well known, the goal of this subsection is to set straight the conventions of our current way to reduce to the DSSYK model correlators from the von Neumann algebraic quantum group.

Starting with the partition function, we notice that, similarly to Section
2.4
, and as discussed in
L2022
, it can be defined by starting with a state of 0 chords, evolving this state over Euclidean time
\beta
, and closing the circle again by taking the inner product with a state of 0 chords. Also, to reduce to the double-scaled SYK mode, we choose the same boundary conditions on the left and right-hand side, in particular
\eta=\xi=1
. We will not always explicitly denote these boundary conditions from now on. Pictorially, the partition function is therefore given by

(142)

where the DSSYK transfer-operator is related to the Casimir as
\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-q^{2}}T=\tilde{\Omega}
and the action of the Casimir operator (
130
) in the rescaled coordinates is given, as discussed earlier, by the recurrence relations for the continuous
q^{2}
-Hermite polynomials, i.e.

\tilde{\Omega}.|k\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-q^{2k+2}}|k+1\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-q^{2k}}|k-1\rangle.

(143)

The eigenvectors of the Casimir operator are given by

|\mathcal{P}^{\theta}\rangle_{\eta,\xi}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\frac{H_{k}(\cos\theta;q^{2})}{\sqrt{(q^{2};q^{2})_{k}}}|k\rangle_{\eta,\xi},

(144)

for
\theta\in[0,\pi]
, where we use standard notations
(\cdot;q)_{\infty}
for the
q
-Pochhammer symbol (
2
) and
H_{k}(\cdot;q)
for the continuous
q
-Hermite polynomial (see for example
KLS2010
), which can be obtained as
a,b\to 0
limit of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomial (
14
).
The vector
|\mathcal{P}^{\theta}\rangle_{\eta,\xi}
has eigenvalue
\cos(\theta)
, and therefore corresponds to a matrix element of the principal unitary series
BK1993
. The inner product of two such states is now calculated as
KLS2010
63
63

63

Alternatively, this also follows from Proposition
A.6
after taking the limit
a,b\to 0
.

\langle\mathcal{P}^{\theta}|\mathcal{P}^{\theta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\eta,\xi}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\frac{H_{k}(\cos\theta;q^{2})H_{k}(\cos\theta^{\prime};q^{2})}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{k}}=\frac{2\pi}{(q^{2},e^{\pm 2i\theta};q^{2})_{\infty}}\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime}),

(145)

where the corresponding Plancherel measure can be read off on the right side via (
24
). It can also be noted that
\langle 0|\mathcal{P}\rangle_{\eta,\xi}=1
. The partition function is therefore given by

\mathcal{Z}(\beta)=\int d\mu(\mathcal{P}^{\theta})d\mu(\mathcal{P}^{\theta^{\prime}})\langle 0|\mathcal{P}^{\theta}\rangle\langle\mathcal{P}^{\theta}|e^{-\beta T}|\mathcal{P}^{\theta^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\mathcal{P}^{\theta^{\prime}}|0\rangle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}(q^{2},e^{\pm 2i\theta};q^{2})_{\infty}e^{-\beta E(\theta)},

(146)

where
E(\theta)=2\cos(\theta)/\sqrt{1-q^{2}}
. This is, of course, exactly the partition function for the double-scaled SYK model
BNS2018
;
BINT2019
(under the redefinition
q^{2}\to q
).

We now continue to calculate the
2
-point functions corresponding to the discrete series. Similarly to Section
2.4
, the operators corresponding to the discrete series should not change the boundary conditions, and we therefore impose that the twisted primitive elements act trivially by their left and right action. In other words, these operators correspond to having quantised momenta equal to zero, i.e.
p_{L}=p_{R}=0
. Using (
128
), this amounts to
\eta=-s^{-1}
and
\xi=t^{-1}
, such that any operator spanned by
\Lambda^{-1}(\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{-s^{-1},y,t^{-1}})
64
64

64

Recall that
\Lambda^{-1}
is well-defined on the compactly supported functions.
leaves the boundary conditions intact. Note that these operators do not have to be elements of the chord subsector. However, it is difficult to explicitly calculate the action of these operators on the chord vectors, and thus, at the moment, we found it impractical to use these for the bilocal operators. Luckily, we already know of another operator on which the twisted primitive elements act trivially, namely the
(s,t)
-spherical element
\rho_{st}
(
92
). Therefore, any function
f(\rho_{st})
(within a reasonable class of functions we consider) defines an operator that does not change the boundary conditions. An important subtlety here is that, in taking the rescaling limit, we also have to rescale this operator, for it to have a well-defined action on the chord states (
139
). That is, we set
\hat{\rho}_{st}=q^{2r}\tilde{\rho}_{st}
(with
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
the normalised spherical element (
107
)) and take the rescaling limit
r\to\infty
at the same rate as for the chord states, i.e.

\hat{\rho}_{st}.|k\rangle\coloneq\lim_{r\to\infty}q^{2r}\tilde{\rho}_{st}.\delta^{-,(st)}_{q^{r},q^{2r}y,q^{r}}=\lim_{r\to\infty}q^{2r}\mu(q^{2r}y)\delta^{-,(st)}_{q^{r},q^{2r}y,q^{r}}=\frac{1}{2}q^{-1}q^{-2k}|k\rangle,

(147)

where we assumed
\eta=\xi=1
and
y=q^{2k+1}
.

Next, we have to figure out which functions
f(\hat{\rho}_{st})
correspond to the discrete series. It is known that the generators
\alpha,\gamma
of the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1))
correspond to matrix elements of the (non-unitary) spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representation (see for example
K1993
). Therefore, a product of
2\ell
generators corresponds to a sum of matrix elements of spin-
\frac{n}{2}
representations with
n\leq 2\ell
. Therefore, for
\ell\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the action of the Casimir operator on powers of the (rescaled) spherical element is given by
\Omega\triangleright(\hat{\rho}_{st})^{\ell}=\left[\ell+1/2\right]_{q}^{2}\cdot(\hat{\rho}_{st})^{\ell}
,
65
65

65

More precisely, for every
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
there exists a polynomial
p_{\ell}(\tilde{\rho}_{st})
of degree
\ell
such that
\Omega\triangleright p_{\ell}(\tilde{\rho}_{st})=\left[\ell+1/2\right]_{q}^{2}\cdot p_{\ell}(\tilde{\rho}_{st})

K1993
. After rescaling
\tilde{\rho}_{st}=q^{-2r}\hat{\rho}_{st}
and taking the limit
r\to\infty
, only the highest order term in the polynomial survives. A similar argument can be made for half-integer values of
\ell
, where some additional care has to be taken with the square root.
where
[\cdot]_{q}
is the
q
-number (
1
). By taking its inverse, it can be directly calculated
66
66

66

Let
X\in\mathcal{U}_{q}
with
\Delta(X)=\sum X_{(1)}\otimes X_{(2)}
, then
X\triangleright(ab)=\sum(X_{(1)}\triangleright a)(X_{(2)}\triangleright b)
. In particular, one can use this ‘Leibniz rule’ to consistently calculate the action of the universal enveloping algebra on the inverses of elements in the coordinate algebra, via
X\triangleright(a\cdot a^{-1})=\sum(X_{(1)}\triangleright a)(X_{(2)}\triangleright a^{-1})=\varepsilon(X)\cdot 1
. Applying this to the action of the Casimir element on the spherical element, although somewhat tedious, gives the result.
that
\Omega\triangleright(\hat{\rho}_{st})^{-\ell}=\left[1/2-\ell\right]_{q}^{2}\cdot(\hat{\rho}_{st})^{-\ell}
, where one can now recognise the Casimir eigenvalue for the discrete series
BK1993
, so that the negative powers of the spherical element correspond to matrix elements of the discrete series. Also, it follows from (

) that the action of
\hat{\rho}_{st}^{-\ell}
on the chord subsector is given by a bounded operator. We therefore define the bilocal operator for the discrete series with
\ell\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
67

Similarly to Section

\widetilde{\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, which at this point has not yet been constructed. However, just as in the classical case, most of the results are expected to follow in close analogy.
at Euclidean time
\tau_{12}=\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}
to be given by

\mathcal{O}^{\ell}(\tau_{12})\coloneq e^{\tau_{12}T}\left[(2q\hat{\rho}_{st})^{-\ell}\right]e^{-\tau_{12}T}.

(148)

We remark that, although
\rho_{st}
generates the
(s,t)
-spherical elements of the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}
, it is not necessarily guaranteed that this operator generates all the ‘spinless’ operators in the von Neumann algebra. To ensure a complete description of the set of operators that are invariant under the actions of the twisted primitive elements, one would therefore have to calculate the action of
\Lambda^{-1}(\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{-s^{-1},y,t^{-1}})
.

Similarly to Section

where

and

, giving the 2-point function in the double-scaled SYK model
BNS2018
;
