.

To compare with what we will consider in the following subsection, let us also recall the Schwarzian limit of this amplitude
MTV2017
. That is, let
\theta_{i}=\pi-|\ln(q^{2})|k_{i}
with
k_{i}\geq 0
and take the limit
q\to 1
. Using (
3
), we can write the overlap between eigenstates as a ratio of
q
-gamma functions, such that

\frac{(q^{4\ell};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q^{2\ell}e^{i(\pm\theta_{1}\pm\theta_{2})};q^{2})_{\infty}}\sim\frac{\Gamma_{q^{2}}(\ell\pm ik_{1}\pm ik_{2})}{\Gamma_{q^{2}}(2\ell)}\xrightarrow{q\to 1}\frac{\Gamma(\ell\pm ik_{1}\pm ik_{2})}{\Gamma(2\ell)}.

(151)

As was observed for the
n
-point functions of the double-scaled SYK model
BNS2018
;
BINT2019
, higher time-ordered (non-crossing) correlators can be calculated by simply inserting extra operators
\mathcal{O}^{\ell}
into the inner product, which gives extra 3j-symbols to integrate over. Similarly (and as in the non-deformed case of Section
2.4
), any out-of-time ordered
n
-point function, i.e. one that has a crossing, will give rise to additional 6j-symbols.

4.3
Strange matter

As discussed at the end of Section
3.3
, the principal unitary series and the discrete series are not the only type of irreducible representations that appear in the decomposition of the regular representations of the von Neumann algebraic quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In particular, a new, non-classical type of irreducible representations that appear in the
q
-deformed Plancherel theorem are the
strange series
. Their appearance can be explained by the fact that for every eigenstate of the Casimir operator
\tilde{\Omega}
with positive eigenvalue, there also is an eigenstate with negative eigenvalue
GKK2010
, whereby the strange series correspond to the ‘negative-energy counterparts’ of the discrete series. The matrix elements of the strange series can be obtained by simply replacing
\ell\to\ell+\frac{i\pi}{2\ln q}

BK1993
.
68
68

68

Indeed, the eigenvalue of
\tilde{\Omega}
for the discrete series is given by
\mu(q^{2\ell-1})
. Under the substitution
\ell\to\ell+\frac{i\pi}{2\ln q}
, this eigenvalue becomes
-\mu(q^{2\ell-1})
, which therefore corresponds to the strange series.
That is, for
\ell\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the bilocal operator corresponding to the strange series is given by

\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\tau_{12})\coloneq e^{\tau_{12}T}\left[(2q\hat{\rho}_{st})^{-\ell-\frac{i\pi}{2\ln q}}\right]e^{-\tau_{12}T}.

(152)

The two-point function for the strange series now follows from (
150
) with the substitution
\ell\to\ell+\frac{i\pi}{2\ln q}
.

Importantly, the overlap between eigenfunctions for the strange series vanishes in the Schwarzian limit. Indeed, consider
\theta_{i}=\pi-|\ln(q^{2})|k_{i}
with
k_{i}\geq 0
, then

\langle\mathcal{P}^{\theta_{1}}|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(0)|\mathcal{P}^{\theta_{2}}\rangle=\frac{(q^{4\ell};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(q^{2\ell}e^{i(\pm\theta_{1}\pm\theta_{2})+i\pi};q^{2})_{\infty}}\sim\frac{\Gamma_{q^{2}}\left(\ell\pm ik_{1}\pm ik_{2}+i\pi/\ln q\right)}{\Gamma_{q^{2}}(2\ell)}\xrightarrow{q\to 1}0.

(153)

This vanishing of the two-point function in this limit is, of course, expected as the strange series does not have a classical analogue. However, let us consider another limit, where we expand one of the angles around the lower part of the spectrum, while the other around the upper part. That is, we take
\theta_{1}=\pi-|\ln(q^{2})|k_{1}
and
\theta_{2}=|\ln(q^{2})|k_{2}
with
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
. It then follows that the
q\to 1
limit gives

\langle\mathcal{P}^{\theta_{1}}|\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(0)|\mathcal{P}^{\theta_{2}}\rangle\sim\frac{\Gamma_{q^{2}}\left(\ell\pm ik_{1}\pm ik_{2}\right)}{\Gamma_{q^{2}}(2\ell)}\xrightarrow{q\to 1}\frac{\Gamma(\ell\pm ik_{1}\pm ik_{2})}{\Gamma(2\ell)},

(154)

such that the strange series has a non-zero amplitude between the lower part and upper part of the spectrum . It was proposed in
BLMPP2025
and
O2025
that zooming into the upper part of the spectrum, i.e.
\theta=|\ln(q^{2})|k
, corresponds to de Sitter JT-gravity.
69
69

69

Notice that different identifications with de Sitter space have been proposed up to date, in particular, by Susskind et al.
S2021
;
S2022
;
S2025
;
Susskind2023sitterspacechordsconfined
;
Sekino2025doublescaledsykqcdflat
and by Verlinde et al.
NV2025
;
V2025
;
VZ2025
;
Tietto2025microscopicmodelsitterspacetime
. The latter proposes that de Sitter space is instead identified with an expansion around
E(\theta)=0
, or
\theta=\pi/2
.
If this proposal were proven to be correct, the strange series would therefore have a non-zero amplitude between the anti-de Sitter and de Sitter regions.

4.4
The one-sided Hilbert space

Let us now discuss the one-sided Hilbert space, i.e. the space of functions with only one fixed boundary condition. Here, we will actually consider a bit more general set of vectors with
e(y)=+1
.
70
70

70

These will in fact be all the vectors with
e(y)=+1
that do not vanish upon rescaling.
In particular, in this section we will consider the subset of vectors
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
where we do not fix
\varepsilon
, but still pick
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\rho_{st}|_{W})\setminus[-1,1]
with
0<y<1
,
\xi=t^{-1}q^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
0<\xi<1
, and
\mu(\eta)\in\sigma(\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R})^{\varepsilon}_{y,\xi}\setminus[-1,1]
with
0<\eta<1
. We then do the same rescaling of variables as in (
136
). Since
\kappa(y)=+1
and
e(y)=+1
, it follows from (
126
) (after rescaling of
y
,
\eta
and
\xi
) that

y=q\eta\xi q^{2k},\quad\eta,\xi<1\quad\text{and}\quad k\in\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}&\text{for }\varepsilon=-1\\
\mathbb{Z}&\text{for }\varepsilon=+1\end{cases}.

(155)

Therefore, for
\varepsilon=+1
, there is no constraint on
y
. Note that we get the same relation to classical coordinates as in (
138
), but now with the constraint
y\leq q\eta\xi
for
\varepsilon=-1
and without any constraints for
\varepsilon=+1
. These states correspond to the top two quadrants in Figure
4
.

Let us fix
\xi\in q^{2\mathbb{Z}}
as our right boundary condition and consider the
restricted
q
-lattice

(\chi,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi)\coloneq\{(-q^{2n},q^{2m+1}):n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\{(q^{2n},q^{2m+1}):n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }q^{2(n+m)}\leq\xi\},

(156)

where we identify
\chi=-\varepsilon\eta^{-1}
. In particular, positive values of
\chi
correspond to vectors with
\varepsilon=-1
, and the constraint
y\leq q\eta\xi
for
\varepsilon=-1
is included in the definition of the lattice. Remember that we stated in Section
2.3
that matrix elements with
p_{L}p_{R}>0
correspond to matrix elements for the normaliser. For the quantum group case, we also see that
\chi\xi>0
corresponds to
\varepsilon=-1
. Next, we turn our vectors into functions on the lattice
\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi)
by considering the basis elements as the functions

\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}\colon\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi)\to\mathbb{C},\quad\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y^{\prime},\xi}(\chi,y)\coloneq(q\xi^{-1}\chi y;q^{2})_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{\eta}(-\varepsilon\chi^{-1})\delta_{y^{\prime}}(y),

(157)

where
\delta_{p}(x)
is the
q^{2}
-delta function (
62
). The inner product on the space of square-integrable functions
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q}(\xi))
is now induced by the orthonormality relations (
122
) of the (rescaled) basis elements
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
, and is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{\xi}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d_{q^{2}}\chi\int_{0}^{\infty}d_{q^{2}}y\ (q\xi^{-1}\chi y;q^{2})_{\infty}\overline{f(\chi,y)}g(\chi,y).

(158)

It can be noted that
(q\xi^{-1}\chi y;q^{2})_{\infty}=0
for
\chi>0
and
|\chi|y\geq q^{-1}\xi
. Therefore, the constraint
y\leq q\eta\xi
for
\varepsilon=-1
is encoded into the measure of the inner product. If we define the shift operator
(\mathcal{T}_{q}.f)(\chi,y)=f(\chi,qy)
,
71
71

71

Although this shift operator itself is not well-defined on the lattice
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q}(\xi)
, the square of this operator is.
then the action of the (non-symmetric) Casimir element (
50
) on this space of functions can be calculated from (
130
) (in the rescaled coordinates) to be equal to

\Omega=\frac{q^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2}+q\mathcal{T}_{q}^{2}-2}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}-\mu y\frac{\chi}{1-q^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2},

(159)

where
\mu\coloneq\xi^{-1}/(1-q^{2})
.

Here, we can recognise a similar action for the Casimir operator as suggested in
(
BINN2023
, (4.10))
for the hands-on lattice realisation of
q
-deformed AdS
2
space. In that paper, the issue of the truncation of the Casimir operator for general realisations of the quantum AdS
2
space was discussed. Namely, the only physically reasonable actions for the Casimir operator are those that truncate, which ensures that there are no additional localised bound states on the Poincaré horizon
BINN2023
. In the von Neumann algebraic description, this truncation is manifest due to the restriction in the
q
-lattice
\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi)
. In the limit
q\to 1
, this action reduces to the classical Casimir action (
27
), where
p_{L}p_{R}<0
corresponds to an action on vectors with
\varepsilon=+1
and
p_{L}p_{R}>0
corresponds to
\varepsilon=-1
. This, therefore, explicitly shows that the Liouville eigenstates (
28
) appear as matrix elements of the normaliser with
\varepsilon=-1
.

Notice that our functions and Casimir operator are currently written in the momentum space, with
\eta
corresponding to momentum. However, at this moment we have not identified an appropriate
q
-Fourier transform that is compatible with the restricted
q
-lattice and the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\xi}
(
158
), and would allow to go back to the coordinate
q
-lattice. Instead, it can be noted that in the limit
\xi\to\infty
, the
q
-lattice
\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)
becomes unconstrained and the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\infty}
is just the ordinary inner product on the
q
-lattice. Moreover, since
\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q}(\infty)
, we can consider the space of square-integrable functions
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q^{2}}(\xi))
as the quotient space

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\xi))=\frac{\{f\colon\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)\to\mathbb{C}:||f||_{\xi}^{2}<\infty\}}{\{f\colon\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)\to\mathbb{C}:||f||_{\xi}^{2}=0\}}.

(160)

We can now proceed as follows: perform a Fourier transform on the space of functions on
\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)
(forgetting about the inner product), and then define the inner product in the position space by imposing Parseval’s formula by hand for arbitrary
\xi
. For the purposes of this paper, we will not write down the explicit form of such a
q
-Fourier transform; various explicit versions of the
q
-Fourier transform can be found in
KS1992
;
R2007
;
BB2007
;
OR1997
;
BINN2023
;
Feinsilver1989qHarmonicAnalysis
. In other words, we assume there exists an invertible
q
-Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}_{q^{2}}\colon\{\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)\to\mathbb{C}\}\to\{\mathbb{R}_{q^{2}}^{2}(\infty)\to\mathbb{C}\},

(161)

such that
\mathcal{D}_{q^{2}}^{\gamma}\left(\mathcal{F}_{q^{2}}f\right)(\gamma,y)=\mathcal{F}_{q^{2}}\left(i\frac{\chi}{1-q^{2}}f\right)(\gamma,y)
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}_{q^{2}}
is the
q^{2}
-derivative in the
\gamma
coordinate as defined under (
57
). We then define the inner product on these functions in position space, imposing Parseval’s formula by hand, that is

\langle\mathcal{F}_{q^{2}}f,\mathcal{F}_{q^{2}}g\rangle_{\xi}\coloneq\langle f,g\rangle_{\xi},

(162)

as an appropriate explicit expression for the inner product in the position basis, which is compatible with the constraint and the inner product in the momentum space, is currently not available to us.

The above construction then defines a space of square-integrable functions on the
q
-lattice
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q^{2}}(\infty)
in the position space
(\gamma,y)
, on which the action of the Casimir operator is given by

\Omega=\frac{q^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2}+q\mathcal{T}_{q}^{2}-2}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}+i\mu y\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2}\mathcal{D}_{q^{2}}^{\gamma},

(163)

where
\mu\coloneq\xi^{-1}/(1-q^{2})
. Here, we can now recognise the same formula for the Casimir operator on
q
-deformed AdS
2
as found in
(
BINN2023
, (4.8))
, which gives the Hamiltonian on lattice AdS
2,q
space. The problems with the Fourier transform that were faced in that paper have now also become apparent in the incompatibility between the Fourier transform and the inner product. In particular, we therefore show that the
q
-deformed AdS
2
lattice is most naturally described in the momentum space, and somewhat ill-behaved in the position space.

4.5
Quantum group action on reduced AdS
2,q

We can now identify the lattice
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{q}(\xi)
as defined in (
156
) for a given
\xi\in q^{2\mathbb{Z}}
with the
reduced quantum AdS
2,q
space
. We refer to this space as reduced, as it corresponds to the space after the rescaling of the coordinates as given in (
136
). The full quantum AdS
2
space would be given in terms of the coordinates before the rescaling. We emphasise here that this
q
-lattice is written in the momentum basis, which is expected to be dual to the coordinate
q
-lattice. For points
(\chi,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi)
, the corresponding classical coordinates are given by

p=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi}{q^{-1}-q},\quad e^{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}y^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(164)

Recall that the space of square-integrable functions
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi))
is given by the inner product as defined in (
158
). Here, one can make the observation that

(q\xi^{-1}\chi y;q^{2})_{\infty}=E_{q^{2}}(-2py\xi^{-1}(1-q^{2})),

(165)

where
E_{q^{2}}(z)=(-z;q^{2})_{\infty}
is the
q^{2}
-exponential
BHS
. In particular, in the limit
q\to 1
where
\xi
would remain fixed, i.e. asymptotic boundary conditions with
p_{R}\sim\lambda^{-1}
, the term in the measure of (
158
) becomes an ordinary exponential, i.e.
E_{q^{2}}(-2py\xi^{-1}(1-q^{2}))\xrightarrow{q\to 1}\exp(-2py\xi^{-1})
. This therefore shows that the space of square-integrable functions over AdS
2
with asymptotic boundary conditions acquires a non-trivial measure, given in terms of the asymptotic boundary condition
\xi
. In the limit
q\to 1
where
p_{R}
is fixed, the exponential vanishes, i.e.
E_{q^{2}}(-2py\xi^{-1}(1-q^{2}))\xrightarrow{q\to 1}1
, and one retrieves the ordinary measure.

Let us now define a different action of the quantum universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
on this space. Firstly, it can be noted that the twisted-primitive element
Y_{s}K
acts diagonally on
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
from the right. Under the rescaling of variables (
136
), we should also rescale the operator to give finite-valued eigenvectors. In particular, we define
\tilde{E}=\frac{1}{2i}q^{r}Y_{s}K
and send
r\to\infty
at the same rate as the rescaled variables. Moreover, we can turn the right action into a left action by taking the adjoint. That is, for a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi))
, we define
\tilde{E}\triangleright f\coloneq f.\tilde{E}^{*}
. In particular, this gives
(\tilde{E}\triangleright f)(\chi,y)=ipf(\chi,y)
, where
p
is defined in (
164
). Inspired by the hands-on lattice realisations of the quantum group action in
BINN2023
, one can now define the full quantum group action on the reduced AdS
2,q
space. Let
(\mathcal{T}_{q}f)(\chi,y)=f(\chi,qy)
,
(\mathcal{R}_{q}f)(\chi,y)=f(q\chi,y)
and
(\mathcal{D}_{q^{2}}^{\chi}f)(\chi,y)=\frac{f(\chi,y)-f(q^{2}\chi,y)}{\chi(1-q^{2})}
, then we define the left action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi))
to be given by

\tilde{K}=\mathcal{T}_{q}\mathcal{R}_{q}^{-1},\quad\tilde{E}=\frac{i}{2}\frac{\chi}{q^{-1}-q},\quad\tilde{F}=2i(\mathcal{D}^{\chi}_{q^{-2}}\mathcal{T}_{q}^{2}-\mathcal{D}^{\chi}_{q^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2})+2iq^{-1}\mu y\mathcal{T}_{q}^{-2},

(166)

where
\mu=\xi^{-1}/(1-q^{2})
. Note here that
\tilde{K}
itself does not map elements from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi))
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{q}^{2}(\xi))
, due to the single shift in
q
, and is therefore not actually a well-defined operator. However,
\tilde{K}^{2}
and
\tilde{K}^{-2}
are well-defined operators. It can be easily verified that these operators satisfy the relations for
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
as given in (
48
).
72
72

72

More precisely,
\tilde{K}^{2}
,
\tilde{E}
and
\tilde{F}
are generators of the Drinfeld-Jimbo presentation of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

QGAR
.
Moreover, the action of the Casimir element (
50
) for these generators coincides with the Casimir action as given in (
159
). The adjoints with respect to the inner product (
158
) are given by
(\tilde{K}^{2})^{*}=\tilde{K}^{-2},\tilde{E}^{*}=-\tilde{E}
and
\tilde{F}^{*}=-\tilde{F}
. In particular, these elements have a
*
-structure of
\mathrm{\mathcal{U}}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}))
. It means that, using the above construction, we have performed a Cayley-like transformation for the quantum group, i.e. redefined the generators to have the
*
-structure of
\mathrm{\mathcal{U}}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}))
instead of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
. The generators
\tilde{K}
,
\tilde{E}
and
\tilde{F}
can therefore be thought of as the quantum group equivalents of the generators in (
18
). However, we emphasise here that, unlike
\tilde{E}
, the actions of the introduced operators
\tilde{K}
and
\tilde{F}
are not guaranteed to be expressible in terms of the generators
E,F,K
of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
. In fact, it is unknown whether an explicit expression for the Cayley transformation, like (
18
), for the quantum group even exists, and even if it does, whether it is unique.

Importantly, the above representation is a representation of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
as an algebra, but not as a Hopf algebra. In particular, the coproduct is not closed among these elements. Indeed, we defined
\tilde{E}
as a rescaling of the twisted primitive element
Y_{s}K
, the coproduct of which is given under (
88
). This implies that
\tilde{E}
has the coproduct
\Delta(\tilde{E})=K^{2}\otimes\tilde{E}+\tilde{E}\otimes 1
. Note here that the Cartan element that appears in this coproduct is the original Cartan element
K
for
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
as defined above (
48
) – it is not
\tilde{K}
. Of course, it cannot be
\tilde{K}
, as then the coproduct would not be a
*
-homomorphism. We remark here that in
BMT2025
, the action of
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
with the
*
-structure of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
was used to compute gravitational matrix elements using Whittaker vectors. However, in that paper, the coproduct of the quantum group was assumed to be
closed
under the generators, giving rise to a
twisted Hopf
*
-algebra

Coquereaux2000TwistedHopf
. We therefore see that this appearance of the twisted Hopf
*
-algebra here can be understood merely as an artefact due to the non-existence of a proper Cayley-transformation of the quantum group on the level of the Hopf algebra. This also implies that one can not easily take tensor products of such representations (
166
), as one has to consider the action of the original Cartan element
K
as well, which might be complicated.

5
Discussion and outlook

In this paper, we reviewed the construction of the von Neumann algebraic quantum group of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, computed its quantum Gauss decomposition and showed that the dynamics of a particle on the von Neumann algebraic quantum homogeneous space AdS
2,q
reduces to the double-scaled SYK model. In Section
2
, when discussing the classical Lie group
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)
and Liouville quantum mechanics, we gave a handful of arguments of why the extension of the global symmetry group to the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is natural. Moreover, when
q
-deforming the symmetry group, it was discussed in Section
3.1
that the locally compact quantum group can in fact only be consistently defined for this normaliser, which can be equivalently regarded as a problem with defining multiplication on the naive (non-extended) deformed group manifold. The reduction to the double-scaled SYK model then appears by restricting to the chord states, which correspond to mixed matrix elements for the normaliser. Considering the normaliser here is an important point. Namely, the eigenstates in the double-scaled SYK model correspond to the continuous
q
-Hermite polynomials, which do not appear as ordinary matrix elements in the representation theory of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)
, but rather in a particular subsector of the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This also elegantly resolves the issue of length positivity, which in other discussions either had to be imposed by hand
BM2024
;
BINN2023
, or required the introduction of a twisted Hopf
*
-algebra with unusual unitarity properties
BMT2025
. For the chord subsector in the von Neumann algebraic quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the chord number is automatically non-negative due to restrictions in the spectra of the twisted primitive elements.

There are several interesting future research directions one can take from here:

•

We have argued in Section
2.3
that for Liouville quantum mechanics, the more natural global symmetry group to consider is the normaliser
\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Indeed, we demonstrated that for the quantum group, the reduction to the double-scaled SYK model works exclusively on the level of the normaliser. However, besides having a natural interpretation for the two-sided wormhole states, the exact role of the normaliser in the quantum theory is not fully understood. For example, as is the case for the quantum group, the non-existence of a global dressing action for the non-compact Poisson-Lie group might also lead to inconsistencies in the classical Poisson-geometric setup
which, to our knowledge, has not been studied. Moreover, using the quantum group derivation, we argued that the wavefunctions for Liouville quantum mechanics can be derived from matrix elements of the normaliser. A corresponding explicit derivation for the classical case, using harmonic analysis on the extended group, still needs to be done cleanly.

•

It is known that the
q\to 1
limit of the double-scaled SYK model, while zooming into the lower edge of the spectrum, reduces to JT gravity on AdS space. Moreover, it has been argued that zooming into other parts of the spectrum, for example, the upper part (
BLMPP2025
;
O2025
) or the middle part (
NV2025
;
V2025
;
VZ2025
), reduces to gravity on 2D or 3D de Sitter space, respectively. Similarly, there are claims that the middle part of the spectrum corresponds to the flat space regime
BLMPP2025
. This would therefore suggest that the quantum homogeneous space of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
somehow at least contains information about both two-dimensional AdS and dS spaces. Although it is known how to reduce to AdS
2
space from its
q
-deformed version, as constructed in Section
4.4
, by taking a
q\to 1
limit, it is not fully understood, to our knowledge, how de Sitter space would appear here. Moreover, supposing that dS space corresponds to the upper part of the spectrum, it was shown in Section
4.3
that the strange series gives non-zero amplitudes between AdS and dS regions, and thus in some sense interpolates between them. It is currently unknown what the correct physics interpretation of such an amplitude would be.

•

To reduce to the double-scaled SYK model, we had to do a rescaling of the coordinates (
136
). Although classically, this rescaling corresponds to a symmetry that preserves the classical Casimir action, the quantum Casimir action does change. In particular, this rescaling was necessary to reduce to the continuous
q
-Hermite polynomials in the
q
-Askey scheme. This suggests that the double-scaled SYK model is in some sense ‘too degenerate’, and it would be natural to expect that a more general bulk theory exists – appropriately to be called the ‘
Askey-Wilson DSSYK
’ (AW-DSSYK) model – which reduces to the double-scaled SYK model in a corresponding scaling limit. In fact, one could readily compute the partition function and the
n
-point functions that such a theory should have by taking the same steps as in Section
4.2
, but starting with the action of the Casimir element before the rescaling, i.e. the one that corresponds to the Askey-Wilson polynomials as opposed to the continuous
q
-Hermite polynomials. On the side of special functions giving eigenstates of the transfer-matrix, this corresponds to going all the way up the
q
-Askey scheme to its highest level. For example, the quantum disk
73
73

73

In our (representation-theoretic) picture, the quantum disk represents another (distinct from the DSSYK coset considered in this paper) quantum homogeneous space of the von Neumann algebraic quantum group, corresponding to its quotient by a maximal compact subgroup.
model
VaksmanQuantumBoundedDomains
reviewed in
Almheiri2025Holographyquantumdisk
has the transfer-matrix eigenstates corresponding to the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials, which are already two levels up in the
q
-Askey scheme. Another possible way to explore this direction is via the connection to the asymmetric exclusion process, see
OkuyamaHolographicTensor2025
and references therein for some recent work.

•

The quantum Gauss decomposition corresponds to the diagonalisation of the square-integrable function with respect to the twisted primitive elements. It was shown at the algebraic level in
S2003
that such a diagonalisation corresponds to a dynamical twist of the quantum group algebra. Indeed, our states obtained dynamical labels
s
and
t
, which change upon action of the quantum group (
124
). In essence, it means that in this paper we have constructed an example of a ‘
von Neumann algebraic dynamical quantum group
’, of which there is currently no clear general mathematical notion. After the rescaling of variables (
136
), this dynamical structure got obscured. However, in, for example, the hypothetical AW-DSSYK model, the dynamical quantum group symmetry should become more apparent. The precise physics implications of such a dynamical symmetry group are currently unknown.

•

We argued in this paper that the harmonic analysis understanding can elegantly clarify subtleties such as length positivity in the DSSYK model. It is well known that harmonic analysis often goes hand in hand with integrability due to the simple fact that having a ‘big enough’ family of commuting self-adjoint operators definitely helps in finding spectral decomposition of the Hilbert space into ‘small enough’ pieces.
The integrable structure of the DSSYK model is rooted in the chord diagrams and corresponding algebras of observables. A more in-depth study of these algebras was initiated in
LinStanfordSymmetryAlgDSSYK
, see also
HVX2025QuantumSymmetry
for recent related work. It would be important to understand the integrable structure behind these harmonic analysis problems in full detail. This should also make a direct contact with the SUSY gauge theory point of view on the DSSYK model suggested in
GaiottoVerline2025SYKSchur
;
GaiottoTeschner2024SchurQuant
, see also
LewisMezei2025Schurconnectionschordcounting
.

•

This paper was devoted to understanding the implications of a specific analytical setting of von Neumann algebraic quantum groups for the physics of the DSSYK model. As mentioned in the introduction, there are further, potentially more refined, setups in non-commutative geometry, such as Connes’ spectral triples
ConnesNoncommutativeGeometry1990
. Exploring these latter can be pictured as (a non-commutative analogue of) going from measure theoretical considerations towards a more precise understanding of the structure of smooth functions on the corresponding space, and is expected to be directly related to a full-fledged (non-commutative-)geometric description of such discretised spacetimes and quantum gravity on those. Among other things, this approach is expected to clarify the geometric nature and dynamics of ‘fat points’ corresponding to infinite-dimensional irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra (see Section
3.1
), whereas the natural metric structure provided by a spectral triple should shed light on the intriguing overlap of correlation functions between AdS and dS regions of the
q
-deformed homogeneous space, which we observed in Section
4.3
. Just like in the von Neumann algebraic quantum group setting, however, the non-compactness of the physics problem in question brings in a lot of technical difficulties. Implementing and exploring this vision in the present context is a work in progress
WPJort
.

•

Finally, in this paper, we analysed the von Neumann algebraic quantum group structure related to the DSSYK, viewed as a toy model of quantum gravity. A natural question is whether one can ‘globalise’ this construction in a physically meaningful way, to get
q
-deformed models with a similar level of analytical control, but richer dynamics. Namely, one can imagine a structure similar to the usual net structure of algebraic QFT
HaagLocalQuantumPhysics
, but having a ‘net’ of
q
-deformed spaces, with symmetries respecting the net structure. A natural way to approach that is by first producing more interesting toy examples from appropriately described glueing of DSSYK-like models, see e.g.
MertensSimonWong2023Proposal3dQG
for some initial work in this direction. Fleshing out this idea in the operator-algebraic framework discussed in the present paper remains an interesting question for the future.

Appendix A
Special functions

In both the derivation of the quantum Gauss decomposition (Section
3.4
) and in the calculation of the partition function and
n
-point functions of the double-scaled SYK model (Section
4
and
BNS2018
;
BINT2019
), one makes heavy use of
q
-orthogonal polynomials/functions. Indeed, their recurrence relations appear in the action of operators in the representation theory of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In this section, we will recall and/or derive some properties of the special functions and give the spectra and eigenvectors of appropriate self-adjoint extensions of some
Jacobi operators
(specific tridiagonal operators) that we encounter in this paper. We refer to
K2001
;
Koelink2018
;
CK2010
for some overviews on Jacobi operators and special functions and to
KLS2010
for an overview on
q
-orthogonal polynomials.

A.1
q
-Calculus and the basic hypergeometric series

Let us begin by recalling some definitions that appear in
q
-calculus and the definition of the basic hypergeometric series. We refer to
QGAR
;
BHS
for complete discussions. During this section, we will keep the deformation parameter in the range
q\in(0,1)
. For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, one can define a
q
-number
as

[z]_{q}\coloneq\frac{q^{z}-q^{-z}}{q-q^{-1}}=\frac{\sinh z\ln q}{\sinh\ln q}.

(1)

It is clear that
[z]_{q}\to z
in the limit
q\to 1
, such that one can indeed interpret this as the
q
-analogue of a number. Such
q
-numbers make a natural appearance in the representation theory of quantum groups, where, for example, upon quantisation, the Casimir eigenvalues are replaced by the corresponding
q
-numbers. Another quantity that makes a natural appearance in representation theory,
q
-combinatorics, and the theory of the basic hypergeometric series is the
q
-Pochhammer symbol
, which is defined by

(z;q)_{n}\coloneq\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1-zq^{k-1}),\quad(z;q)_{0}\coloneq 1,\quad(z;q)_{-n}\coloneq(zq^{-n};q)_{n}^{-1},

(2)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We also define the limit
(z;q)_{\infty}\coloneq\lim_{n\to\infty}(z;q)_{n}
and the product
(z_{1},...,z_{r};q)_{n}\coloneq(z_{1};q)_{n}\cdots(z_{r},q)_{n}
. We will also occasionally make use of the notation
(z^{\pm};q)_{n}\coloneq(z,z^{-1};q)_{n}
. The
q
-Pochhammer symbol is closely related to the factorial, and therefore also to the gamma function. Indeed, the
q
-Pochhammer symbols allows us to define a
q
-analogue of the gamma function, called the
q
-gamma function
, which is given by

\Gamma_{q}(z)\coloneq(1-q)^{1-z}\frac{(q,q)_{\infty}}{(q^{z};q)_{\infty}}.

(3)

It can be shown that the
q
-gamma function reduces to the ordinary gamma function in the limit
q\to 1
, that is
\Gamma_{q}(z)\to\Gamma(z)
as
q\to 1

QGAR
. Some of the properties that the
q
-Pochhammer symbol and the
q
-gamma functions have can be found in
QGAR
;
BHS
.

The
basic hypergeometric series
are defined by
BHS

{}_{r}\varphi_{s}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a_1,...,a_r}{b_1,...,b_s};q,z\right):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a_{1},...,a_{r};q)_{n}}{(b_{1},...,b_{s};q)_{n}}\left((-1)^{n}q^{n(n-1)/2}\right)^{1+s-r}\frac{z^{n}}{(q;q)_{n}},

(4)

for
r,s\geq 0
. These are the natural
q
-analogue of the hypergeometric series, and can therefore be used to solve
q
-differential equations (i.e. certain recurrence relations). Here, it can be verified using the definition of the
q
-Pochhammer symbol that the above series terminates if one of the
a_{i}
’s equals
q^{-N}
for some
N\geq 1
. Moreover, the series converges for all
z
if
r\leq s
and for
|z|<1
if
r=s+1
. The basic hypergeometric series (and their analytical continuations), together with the
q
-Pochhammer symbols, will be our main objects in defining eigenfunctions of some Jacobi operators.

A.2
Little
q
-Jacobi functions

The
little
q
-Jacobi functions
were originally defined to describe a kind of Fourier transform that appears in the representation theory of the quantum group
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)

Kakehi1995
;
KS2001
. We refer to these papers, as well as
K2001
and
GKK2010
, for discussions on these functions. Here, we will give some of the basic results. Consider the unbounded doubly-infinite Jacobi operator
L
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
given by

\begin{gathered}Le_{k}\coloneq a_{k}e_{k+1}+b_{k}e_{k}+a_{k-1}e_{k-1}\\
a_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{q^{-k}}{r}\right)\left(1-\frac{cq^{-k}}{d^{2}r}\right)},\quad b_{k}=\frac{q^{-k}(c+q)}{2dr},\end{gathered}

(5)

where
c,d,r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a_{k}>0
74
74

74

More generally, the coefficients can also be complex-valued (see, for example,
KS2001
) under the condition that
a_{k}>0
and
b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
. All the same results will follow. However, some points in the discrete spectrum might vanish if the values in (
8
) fail to remain real-valued.
. Let
y\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mu(y)\coloneq\frac{1}{2}(y+y^{-1})
. Here, one can recognise the recurrence relation for the little
q
-Jacobi functions, which are defined by

f_{k}(y)\coloneq f_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)=d^{k}\sqrt{\frac{(cq^{1-k}/d^{2}r;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{1-k}/r;q)_{\infty}}}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{dy, d/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right).

(6)

The vector
f(y)\coloneq\sum_{k}f_{k}(y)e_{k}
is then an eigenvector of
L
with eigenvalue
\mu(y)
. Moreover, we can normalise this vector by rescaling the little
q
-Jacobi functions and defining

j(x;c,d;q,r)=\|f(x;c,d;q,r)\|^{-1}f(x;c,d;q,r),

(7)

where this norm is explicitly given by
(
GKK2010
, §B.5)
. Of course, not all possible values
\mu(y)
will appear in the spectrum of the self-adjoint extension of
L
. Instead, we define the set

D=\left[\left\{d^{-1}q^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{d}{c}q^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\}\cup\left\{rdq^{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}\right]\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}:|y|<1\}.

(8)

and the measure on
\mathbb{R}
given by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)d\nu(x)\coloneq\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)dx+\sum_{x\in\mu(D)}f(x),

(9)

which contains both a continuous and a discrete support. In particular, the support of this measure is exactly given by the spectrum of the Jacobi operator
L
. In fact, we now have the following result:

A.1 Proposition

(
(
GKK2010
, §B.5)
)
.

The operator
L
is essentially self-adjoint for
0<c\leq q^{2}
. Moreover, for
0<c<1
,
L
has a self-adjoint extension with spectrum given by
\sigma(L)=[-1,1]\cup\mu(D)
and spectral measure given by

\langle u|E_{L}(\Delta)|v\rangle=\int_{\Delta}\overline{(\mathcal{F}_{L}u)(x)}(\mathcal{F}_{L}v)(s)d\nu(x),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{L}u)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}j_{k}(x)

for any Borel subset
\Delta\subset\sigma(L)
and
u,v\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The above proposition, therefore, gives a Fourier transform that corresponds to the operator
L
. Let us now discuss some additional properties of the little
q
-Jacobi functions. Firstly, the little
q
-Jacobi functions satisfy the symmetry relation
(
GKK2010
, (B.46))

j_{k}(x;c,d;q,r)=j_{k}(x;c,c/d;q,rd^{2}/c).

(10)

Moreover, it satisfies the shift condition
(
GKK2010
, (B.46))

j_{k}(x;c,d;q,q^{p}r)=\mathrm{sgn}(d)^{p}j_{k+p}(x;c,d;q,r),

(11)

where it should be noted that we use a slightly different convention than
GKK2010
. Under the redefinition
j_{k}\to j_{-k}
, we get the same expression. Next, we will state some useful
q
-difference relations for the little
q
-Jacobi functions. Firstly, we recall the result from
GKK2010
:

A.2 Lemma

(
(
GKK2010
, Lemma B.16)
)
.

The functions
j_{k}(x;c,d;q,r)
satisfy

\begin{split}&\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{r}q^{-k}}j_{k+1}(x;c,d;q,r)-d\sqrt{1-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}q^{-k}}j_{k}(x;c,d;q,r)\\
&=\mathrm{sgn}(d)\sqrt{1-2xd+d^{2}}j_{k}(x;qc,qd;q,r).\end{split}

Next, we give some new
q
-difference relations:

A.3 Lemma
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

\begin{split}&q^{\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}q^{-k}}j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\\
&=A(y)j_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r)+A(y^{-1})j_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\\
\end{split}

where
A(y)=\sqrt{-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}\frac{(1-dy)(1-qdy/c)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Proof.

Firstly, by using the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
), it can be shown that

{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{dy, d/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right)=\frac{1-dy}{1-y^{2}}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{qdy, d/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right)+\frac{1-d/y}{1-1/y^{2}}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{dy, qd/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right).

Therefore, by definition of
f_{k}
as in (
6
), we have

\begin{split}&q^{\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}q^{-k}}f_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\\
&=\frac{1-dy}{1-y^{2}}f_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r)+\frac{1-d/y}{1-1/y^{2}}f_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r).\end{split}

To obtain the result, one uses the normalisation of the little
q
-Jacobi functions as given in
(
GKK2010
, (B.37))
. For example, for
y=q^{1-\ell}/dr
, we have

\|f(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\|^{2}=\frac{(q,q,cy/d,dy,c/dy,d/y;q)_{\infty}}{-(1-y^{2})(dr)^{2(1-\ell)}q^{-(\ell-2)(\ell-1)}}.

(12)

Therefore also

\begin{split}\frac{\|f(\mu(q^{1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\|^{2}}{\|f(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\|^{2}}&=-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}\frac{(1-y^{2})(1-qdy/c)}{(1-qy^{2})(1-dy)},\\
\frac{\|f(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);c,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\|^{2}}{\|f(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\|^{2}}&=-\frac{c}{d^{2}r}\frac{(1-1/y^{2})(1-qd/cy)}{(1-q/y^{2})(1-d/y)}.\end{split}

One can find the same expressions for other values of
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)\setminus[-1,1]
. Now, using the fact that
j_{k}=\frac{1}{\|f_{k}\|}f_{k}
, the result follows.
∎

By using the symmetry relation (
10
) and (
11
), one also gets the second relation

A.4 Corollary
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

\begin{split}&q^{\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{r}q^{-k}}j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\\
&=A(y)j_{k+1}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);c,q^{-1/2}d;q,r)+A(y^{-1})j_{k+1}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);c,q^{-1/2}d;q,r)\\
\end{split}

where
A(y)=\mathrm{sgn}(d)\sqrt{-\frac{1}{r}\frac{(1-cy/d)(1-qy/d)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Lastly, there is a third
q
-difference equation that will be useful.

A.5 Lemma
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

q^{\frac{k}{2}}j_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)=A(y)j_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r)+A(y^{-1})j_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r),

where
A(y)=\sqrt{\frac{qy}{dr}\frac{(1-dy)(1-cy/d)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof for
Lemma
A.3
. However, here one starts with the relation

\begin{split}&(1-qdy/c){}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{dy, d/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right)\\
&=(1-dy){}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{qdy, d/y}{c};q,rq^{k}\right)-\frac{qdy}{c}(1-q^{-1}c){}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{dy, d/y}{q^{-1}c};q,rq^{k}\right),\end{split}

which can be derived from the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
). Applying these relations to the functions
f_{k}
as defined in (
6
) gives

\begin{split}q^{\frac{k}{2}}f_{k}(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)&=\frac{(1-dy)(1-cy/d)}{1-y^{2}}f_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\\
&+\frac{(1-d/y)(1-c/dy)}{1-1/y^{2}}f_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r).\end{split}

We now again use the normalisation of the little
q
-Jacobi functions as given in
(
GKK2010
, (B.37))
. For example, for
y=q^{1-\ell}/dr
, this normalisation is given in (
12
), and therefore

\begin{split}\frac{\|f(\mu(q^{1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\|^{2}}{\|f(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\|^{2}}&=\frac{qy}{dr}\frac{(1-y^{2})}{(1-qy^{2})(1-dy)(1-cy/d)}\\
\frac{\|f(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);qc,q^{1/2}d;q,r)\|^{2}}{\|f(\mu(y);c,d;q,r)\|^{2}}&=\frac{q}{dry}\frac{(1-1/y^{2})}{(1-q/y^{2})(1-d/y)(1-c/dy)}.\end{split}

One can find the same expressions for other values of
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)\setminus[-1,1]
. Now using, the fact that
j_{k}=\frac{1}{\|f\|}f_{k}
, the result follows.
∎

All the above lemmas can be extended to hold for all
\mu(y)\in\sigma(L)
, i.e. also for
\mu(y)\in[-1,1]
. One has to be careful here with the fact that now
\mu(q^{1/2}y)
and
\mu(q^{-1/2}y)
do not necessarily lie in the spectrum of
L
. However, the functions
j_{k}
can be naturally extended to these values.

A.3
Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials

In this section, we consider the
Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials
. These are actually very closely related to the little
q
-Jacobi functions, and one might view them as some bounded variant. Indeed, as can be seen in Section
3.4
, the appearance of the little
q
-Jacobi function is often accompanied by the appearance of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials. For
0<q<1
, consider the bounded Jacobi operator
J_{q}
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
given by

\begin{gathered}J_{q}e_{k}\coloneq a_{k}e_{k+1}+b_{k}e_{k}+a_{k-1}e_{k-1},\\
a_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1-q^{k+1})(1-abq^{k})},\quad b_{k}=\frac{1}{2}q^{k}(a+b),\end{gathered}

(13)

where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
75
75

75

More generally,
a
and
b
can also be complex conjugates such that
\text{max}(|a|,|b|)<1
. In this case, the same results follow, but the discrete spectrum will vanish.
such that
ab<1
. Again, one can observe the similarity to (
5
). This operator is given by the (symmetric) recurrence relation for the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (see
(
KLS2010
, §14.8)
or
(
GKK2010
, §B.4)
), which are given by

\begin{split}P_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)&=a^{-k}(ab;q)_{k}~{}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, ay, a/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)\\
&=(a/y;q)_{k}y^{k}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, by}{q^{1-k}y/a};q,q/ya\right),\end{split}

(14)

for
y\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mu(y)=\frac{1}{2}(y+y^{-1})
. These polynomials only solve for the non-symmetric recurrence relation. The polynomials that solve the symmetric recurrence relation are given by

p_{k}(x)\coloneq p_{k}(x;a,b;q)=(q,ab;q)_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}P_{k}(x;a,b;q),

(15)

such that
p(x)=\sum_{k}p_{k}(x)e_{k}
are eigenvectors of
L
with eigenvalue
x
. These vectors can be normalised by defining

h(x;a,b;q)\coloneq\|p(x;a,b;q)\|^{-1}p(x;a.b;q),

(16)

where the norm is given in
(
GKK2010
, §B.4)
. Not all values of
x
appear in the self-adjoint extension of
J_{q}
. Instead, we define the set

D_{q}\coloneq\left[\{a^{-1}q^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\cup\{b^{-1}q^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\right]\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}:|y|<1\},

(17)

and the measure on
\mathbb{R}
given by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)d\nu(x)\coloneq\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)dx+\sum_{x\in\mu(D_{q})}f(x).

(18)

The support of this measure now corresponds to the spectrum of the operator
J_{q}
. Indeed, we have the following result:

A.6 Proposition

(
(
GKK2010
, §B.4)
)
.

J_{q}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator with spectrum
\sigma(J_{q})=[-1,1]\cup\mu(D_{q})
. Moreover, its spectral measure is given by

\langle u|E_{J_{q}}(\Delta)|v\rangle=\int_{\Delta}\overline{(\mathcal{F}_{J_{q}}u)(x)}(\mathcal{F}_{J_{q}}v)(x)d\nu(x),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{J_{q}}u)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}u_{k}h_{k}(x),

for any Borel subset
\Delta\subset\sigma(J_{q})
and
u,v\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.

Next, we are also interested in the unbounded case. That is, we replace
q\to q^{-1}
and therefore consider the unbounded Jacobi operator
J_{q^{-1}}
. Here, we assume
ab>1
. The eigenfunctions of this Jacobi operator are then given by
{q}^{-1}
-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (i.e. replace
q\to q^{-1}
in (
15
)), which are actually dual to the little
q
-Jacobi polynomials (not functions), see
(
G2004
, Remark 3.1)
or
K2004
. The spectrum for the self-adjoint extension of
J_{q^{-1}}
will not simply be given by replacing
q\to q^{-1}
in the spectrum of
J_{q}
. Instead, we consider the discrete set

D_{q^{-1}}\coloneq\{aq^{-k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

(19)

On this discrete set, we define the functions

h_{k}(\mu(aq^{-n});a,b;q^{-1})=\sqrt{\frac{q^{k^{2}}w_{n}(a,b;q)}{(ab)^{k}(q,(ab)^{-1};q)_{k}}}P_{k}(\mu(aq^{-n});a,b;q^{-1}),

(20)

where

w_{n}(a,b;q)=\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{n}q^{n^{2}}\frac{1-a^{-2}q^{2n}}{1-a^{-2}}\frac{(a^{-2},(ab)^{-1};q)_{n}}{(q,a^{-1}bq;q)_{n}}\frac{(a^{-1}bq;q)_{\infty}}{(qa^{-2};q)_{\infty}}.

(21)

Since
J_{q^{-1}}
is symmetric in
a
and
b
, we can assume w.l.o.g that
a/b>q
. In this case, the functions
h_{k}
form an orthonormal set and satisfy
K2004

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(h_{k}h_{\ell})(\mu(aq^{-n});a,b;q^{-1})=\delta_{k\ell}.

(22)

This then gives the following result

A.7 Proposition

(
K2004
)
.

J_{q^{-1}}
is essentially self-adjoint for
a/b>q^{-1}
. Moreover, for
a/b>q
, there exists a self-adjoint extension of
J_{q^{-1}}
with spectrum
J_{q^{-1}}=\{\mu(a^{-1}q^{k}):k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
and spectral measure

\langle u|E_{J_{q^{-1}}}(\Delta)|v\rangle=\sum_{x\in\Delta}\overline{(\mathcal{F}_{J_{q^{-1}}}u)(x)}\mathcal{F}_{J_{q^{-1}}}v(x),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{J_{q^{-1}}}u)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}u_{k}h_{k}(x;a,b;q^{-1}),

for any Borel subset
\Delta\subseteq\sigma(J_{q^{-1}})
and
u,v\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.

Let us now discuss some relations on the functions
h_{k}
corresponding to the
q
-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (
16
). Similar relations for the
q^{-1}
-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials can be found by simply replacing
q\to q^{-1}
. Firstly, we have

A.8 Lemma

(
(
GKK2010
, Lemma B.14)
)
.

The functions
h_{k}(x;a,b;q)
satisfy

\sqrt{1-abq^{k}}h_{k}(x;a,qb;q)-b\sqrt{1-q^{k}}h_{k-1}(x;a,bq;q)=\sqrt{1-2bx+b^{2}}h_{k}(x;a,b;q),

for
x\in\sigma(J_{q})
.

We now derive some additional
q
-difference equations.

A.9 Lemma
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(J_{q})\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
h_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

\begin{split}&q^{-\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-abq^{k}}~h_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)\\
&=A(y)h_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q)+A(y^{-1})h_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q)\\
\end{split}

where
A(y)=\sqrt{-qy^{2}\frac{(1-ay)(1-by)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Proof.

Firstly, by the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
), we have

\begin{split}&(1-qy/b){}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, ay, a/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)\\
&=(1-ay){}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, qay, a/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)-\frac{qy}{b}(1-q^{-1}ab){}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, ay, a/y}{q^{-1}ab, 0};q,q\right).\end{split}

From the definition of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials in (
15
) this now gives

\begin{split}q^{-\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-abq^{k}}p_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)&=\frac{(1-ay)(1-by)}{(1-y^{2})\sqrt{1-ab}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q)\\
&+\frac{(1-a/y)(1-b/y)}{(1-1/y^{2})\sqrt{1-ab}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q).\end{split}

We can now use the normalisation of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials as given in
(
GKK2010
, (B.18))
. In particular, for
y=aq^{\ell}
, we have

\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}=\frac{(b/a;q)_{\infty}(q,aq/b;q)_{\ell}(1-a^{2})}{(a^{-2};q)_{\infty}(a^{2},ab;q)_{\ell}(1-a^{2}q^{2\ell})}q^{\ell^{2}}a^{3\ell}b^{\ell}.

(23)

This gives

\begin{split}\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=-qy^{2}\frac{(1-y^{2})(1-ab)}{(1-ay)(1-by)(1-qy^{2})}\\
\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=-qy^{-2}\frac{(1-1/y^{2})(1-ab)}{(1-a/y)(1-b/y)(1-q/y^{2})}.\end{split}

By using that
\|h_{k}\|=\frac{1}{\|p\|}p_{k}
, this gives the result.
∎

A.10 Lemma
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(J_{q})\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
h_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

\begin{split}&q^{-\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-q^{k}}~h_{k-1}(\mu(y);a,b;q)\\
&=A(y)h_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)+A(y^{-1})h_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\\
\end{split}

where
A(y)=\sqrt{-q^{-1}aby^{2}\frac{(1-qy/a)(1-qy/b)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Proof.

This proof is analogous to the proof of
Lemma
A.9
. By the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
), we have

q^{-k}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, by}{q^{1-k}y/a};q,q/ya\right)={}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, by}{q^{1-k}y/a};q,1/ya\right)-(1-q^{-k}){}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{1-k}, by}{q^{1-k}y/a};q,q/ya\right)

From the definition of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials in (
15
), this now gives

\begin{split}q^{-\frac{k}{2}}\sqrt{1-q^{k}}p_{k-1}(\mu(y);a,b;q)&=\frac{y\sqrt{1-q^{-1}ab}}{1-y^{2}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\\
&+\frac{y^{-1}\sqrt{1-q^{-1}ab}}{1-1/y^{2}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q).\end{split}

We can now again use the normalisation of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials as given in
(
GKK2010
, (B.18))
and (
23
). This then gives

\begin{split}\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=-q^{-1}ab\frac{(1-y^{2})(1-qy/a)(1-qy/b)}{(1-qy^{2})(1-q^{-1}ab)}\\
\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{-1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=-q^{-1}ab\frac{(1-1/y^{2})(1-q/ay)(1-q/by)}{(1-q/y^{2})(1-q^{-1}ab)}.\end{split}

By using the fact that
\|h_{k}\|=\frac{1}{\|p\|}p_{k}
, this gives the result.
∎

A.11 Lemma
.

For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(J_{q})\setminus[-1,1]
, the functions
h_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)
satisfy the
q
-difference relations

\begin{split}&q^{-\frac{k}{2}}h_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)\\
&=A(y)h_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)+A(y^{-1})h_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\\
\end{split}

where
A(y)=\sqrt{by\frac{(1-ay)(1-qy/b)}{(1-y^{2})(1-qy^{2})}}
.

Proof.

This proof is again analogous to the previous proofs. By the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
23
), we have

\begin{split}&{}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, ay, a/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)\\
&=\frac{1-ay}{1-y^{2}}{}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, qay, a/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)-\frac{1-a/y}{1-1/y^{2}}{}_{3}\varphi_{2}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, ay, qa/y}{ab, 0};q,q\right)\end{split}

From the definition of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials in (
15
), this now gives

\begin{split}q^{-\frac{k}{2}}p_{k}(\mu(y);a,b;q)&=\frac{1-ay}{1-y^{2}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\\
&+\frac{1-a/y}{1-1/y^{2}}p_{k}(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q).\end{split}

Using the normalisation of the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials as given in
(
GKK2010
, (B.18))
and (
23
), this gives

\begin{split}\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=by\frac{(1-y^{2})(1-qy/b)}{(1-qy^{2})(1-ay)}\\
\frac{\|p(\mu(q^{-1/2}y);q^{1/2}a,q^{-1/2}b;q)\|^{2}}{\|p(\mu(y);a,b;q)\|^{2}}&=by^{-1}\frac{(1-1/y^{2})(1-q/by)}{(1-q/y^{2})(1-a/y)}.\end{split}

By using the fact that
\|h_{k}\|=\frac{1}{\|p\|}p_{k}
, this gives the result.
∎

Again, by replacing
q\to q^{-1}
, the above relations give the
q
-difference equations for the
q^{-1}
-Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials. Also, the above lemmas can be extended to hold for all
\mu(y)\in\sigma(J_{q})
, so also for
\mu(y)\in[-1,1]
. However, one has to be careful with the fact that
\mu(q^{1/2}y)
and
\mu(q^{-1/2}y)
are not part of the spectrum of
J_{q}
. The functions
h_{k}
need to be extended to these values for the relations to make sense.

A.4
Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials

Another polynomial that shows up in the representation theory of
\mathrm{SU}_{q}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and actually only in the normaliser, is the
Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomial

AlSalamCarlitz1965
. Consider the Jacobi operator
J
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
given by

\begin{gathered}Je_{k}=a_{k}e_{k+1}+b_{k}e_{k}+a_{k-1}e_{k},\\
a_{k}=q^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{1-q^{k+1}},\quad b_{k}=\varepsilon(a^{\frac{1}{2}}+a^{-\frac{1}{2}})q^{-k},\end{gathered}

(24)

where
0<a<q^{-1}
and
\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}
. This Jacobi operator is indeed exactly given by the (symmetric) recurrence relation for the Al-Salam-Carlitz polynomials of type II
(
KLS2010
, §14.25)
, which are given by

P_{k}^{(a)}(x;q)\coloneq(-a)^{k}q^{-\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)}{}_{2}\varphi_{0}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{q^{-k}, x}{-};q,q^{k}/a\right).

(25)

In order for these to define an eigenvector for
J
, the polynomial should be symmetrised. In fact, we will also immediately normalise these functions. The symmetrised and normalised Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials are then given by

p_{k}^{a}(\varepsilon q^{-n}/\sqrt{a};q)\coloneq\sqrt{\frac{q^{n^{2}+k^{2}}a^{n-k}(aq;q)_{\infty}}{(q;q)_{n}(q;q)_{k}(aq;q)_{n}}}\varepsilon^{k}P_{k}^{(a)}(q^{-n};q),

(26)

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. The vector
p^{a}(x)=\sum_{k}p^{a}_{k}(x)e_{k}
is now an eigenfunction of
J
with eigenvalue
x
for
x=\varepsilon q^{-n}/\sqrt{a}
. Moreover, they satisfy the orthonormality relation
(
KLS2010
, §14.25)

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}p_{k}^{a}(\varepsilon q^{-n}/\sqrt{a};q)p_{\ell}^{a}(\varepsilon q^{-n}/\sqrt{a};q)=\delta_{k\ell}

(27)

for
0<a<q^{-1}
. We now get the following result:

A.12 Proposition

(
Chihara1986
)
.

J
is essentially self-adjoint for
0<a<q
. For
0<a<q^{-1}
, there exists a self-adjoint extension of
J
with spectrum
\sigma(J)=\{\varepsilon q^{-k}/\sqrt{a}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
and spectral measure

\langle u|E_{J}(\Delta)|v\rangle=\sum_{x\in\Delta}\overline{(\mathcal{F}_{J}u)(x)}(\mathcal{F}_{J}v)(x),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{J}u)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}u_{k}p_{k}^{a}(x;q)

for any Borel set
\Delta\subseteq\sigma(J)
and
u,v\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.

These polynomials satisfy the following useful
q
-difference equation:

A.13 Lemma
.

For
x\in\sigma(J)
, the functions
p_{k}^{a}
satisfy the
q
-difference equation

q^{\frac{k}{2}}p_{k}^{a}(x)=q^{\frac{1}{2}}|x|^{-1}\sqrt{q^{-1}|x|/\sqrt{a}-1}p_{k}^{qa}(q^{-1/2}x)+q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x|^{-1}\sqrt{|x|\sqrt{a}-1}p_{k}^{qa}(q^{1/2}x).

Proof.

Define
{}_{2}\varphi_{0}^{a}(x)={}_{2}\varphi_{0}\left(q^{-k},x;-:q,q^{k}/a\right)
. Then, using the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
), this function satisfies the
q
-difference relation

(1-x){}_{2}\varphi_{0}^{a}(qx)+x{}_{2}\varphi_{0}^{q^{-1}a}(x)={}_{2}\varphi_{0}^{a}(x).

Therefore, from the definition of the Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials (
25
), these satisfy

(1-q^{-n})P_{k}^{(a)}(q^{1-n})+q^{-n}q^{k}P_{k}^{(q^{-1}a)}(q^{-n})=P_{k}^{(a)}(q^{-n}).

If we write
x=\varepsilon q^{-n}/\sqrt{a}
, then

\begin{split}\|P^{(q^{-1}a)}(q^{-n})\|^{2}&=-q^{-k}\frac{a^{-1}}{1-\varepsilon x/\sqrt{a}}\|P^{(a)}(q^{-n})\|.\end{split}

Also note that
P_{k}^{(q^{-1}a)}(q^{-n})\sim p_{k}^{q^{-1}a}(q^{1/2}x)
. Combining these, one can write the
q
-difference equation for
p
, and after some rewriting, one gets the result.
∎

A.5
Ciccoli-Koelink-Koornwinder functions

We will now consider a set of functions of which the recurrence relation may be regarded as a non-terminating version of the recurrence for the Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials (
24
), which were first discussed in the paper by Ciccoli, Koelink and Koornwinder
CKK1999
. Consider the doubly-infinite Jacobi operator
L
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
given by

\begin{gathered}Le_{k}\coloneq a_{k}e_{k+1}+b_{k}e_{k}+a_{k-1}e_{k-1},\\
a_{k}=q^{-\frac{1}{2}(k+1)}\sqrt{1+c^{-1}q^{-k}},\quad b_{k}=\sqrt{c^{-1}}(t+t^{-1})q^{-k},\end{gathered}

(28)

where
c>0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
such that
a_{k}>0
and
b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
. For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we consider the functions

\begin{split}V_{k}^{t}(z)&=q^{\frac{1}{2}k}(-t)^{-k}(-cq^{k};q)_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-qz / \sqrt{c} t, 0}{qt^{-2}};q,-cq^{k}\right)\\
&=\frac{(-qz/\sqrt{c}t;q)_{\infty}}{(qt^{-2};q)_{\infty}}q^{\frac{1}{2}k}(-t)^{-k}(-cq^{k};q)_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{-\sqrt{c} / tz, -cq^k}{0};q,-qz/\sqrt{c}t\right),\end{split}

(29)

then
V^{t}(z)=\sum_{k}V_{k}^{t}(z)e_{k}
is an eigenvector of
L
with eigenvalue
z
with norm given by
(
CKK1999
, (3.2), (3.4))

\|V^{t}(z)\|^{2}=\frac{t}{\sqrt{c}|z|}\frac{(-qz/\sqrt{c}t;q)_{\infty}}{(-qtz/\sqrt{z};q)_{\infty}}\frac{(q,q,-c/t^{2},-qt^{2}/c;q)_{\infty}}{(-c,-q/c,qt^{-2},qt^{-2};q)_{\infty}}.

(30)

Therefore, we will define the normalised functions to be equal to
\tilde{V}^{t}_{k}(z)=\|V^{t}(z)\|^{-1}V^{t}_{k}(z)
. For
t^{-2}=q^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>-1
and
z=-\sqrt{c}t^{-1}q^{n}
with
n\geq 0
, these functions are dual to the
q
-Laguerre functions (see
CKK1999
). We now have the following result for the Jacobi operator
L
:

A.14 Proposition

(
CKK1999
)
.

L
is essentially self-adjoint for
|t|>q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. For
|t|>q^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
L
has a self-adjoint extension with spectrum

\sigma(L)=\{-\sqrt{c}t^{-1}q^{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\cup\{\sqrt{c^{-1}}tq^{k}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\{0\}

and the spectral measure given by

\langle u|E_{L}(\Delta)|v\rangle=\sum_{x\in\Delta}\overline{(\mathcal{F}_{L}u)(x)}(\mathcal{F}_{L}v)(x),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{L}u)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x;c;q)

for any Borel set
\Delta\subseteq\sigma(L)
and
u,v\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The functions
\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x)
now satisfy a few useful relations:

A.15 Lemma
.

Let
x\in\sigma(L)
, then the functions
\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}
satisfy the relations

\sqrt{1+c^{-1}q^{1-k}}\tilde{V}_{k-1}^{t}(x)+\sqrt{c^{-1}}~tq^{-\frac{1}{2}k}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x)=\sqrt{1+xt/\sqrt{c}}~\tilde{V}_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}x)

and

\sqrt{c}\sqrt{1+c^{-1}q^{-k}}~\tilde{V}_{k+1}^{t}(x)+t^{-1}q^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}k}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x)=-\sqrt{c}\sqrt{1+qx/\sqrt{c}t}~\tilde{V}_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{1/2}x).

Proof.

Firstly, it can be shown from the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
) that (see also
(
BHS
, ex. 1.9)
)

{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{aq, b}{0};q,z\right)-{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a, b}{0};q,z\right)=az(1-b){}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{aq, bq}{0};q,z\right).

From the definition (
29
), it then follows that
V_{k}^{t}(z)
satisfies the relation

\sqrt{1+c^{-1}q^{1-k}}V_{k-1}^{t}(z)+\sqrt{c^{-1}}tq^{-\frac{1}{2}k}V_{k}^{t}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{c}t^{-1}+z}{1-qt^{-2}}V_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}z).

Moreover, using the normalisation (
30
) of the functions, we have

\|V^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}z)\|^{2}=\frac{(1-qt^{-2})^{2}}{(1+tz/\sqrt{c})}\frac{t^{2}}{c}\|V^{t}(z)\|^{2},

which gives the first relation. The second relation follows analogously, but now starting from the contiguous relation

{}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{0};q,z\right)-(1-b)\ {}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,bq}{0};q,z\right)=b\ {}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{a,b}{0};q,qz\right)

and then performing the same steps as before.
∎

A.16 Lemma
.

For
x\in\sigma(L)
, the functions
\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}
satisfy the
q
-difference equation

q^{\frac{k}{2}}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x)=x^{-1}\sqrt{1+tx/\sqrt{c}}~\tilde{V}_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}x)-x^{-1}\sqrt{q^{-1}+x/\sqrt{c}t}\tilde{V}_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{1/2}x).

Proof.

Define
{}_{2}\varphi_{1}^{t}(z)={}_{2}\varphi_{1}\left(-qz/\sqrt{c}t,0;qt^{-2}:q,-cq^{k}\right)
.
From the definition of the basic hypergeometric series (
4
), it then follows that

(1+qz/\sqrt{c}t){}_{2}\varphi_{1}^{t}(qz)+qzt/\sqrt{c}(1-t^{-2}){}_{2}\varphi_{1}^{q^{1/2}t}(q^{1/2}z)=(1+qzt/\sqrt{c}){}_{2}\varphi_{1}^{t}(z).

From (
29
) and after some rewriting, it then follows that

(1+qz/\sqrt{c}t)V_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{1/2}z)+zt/\sqrt{c}(1-qt^{-2})q^{\frac{1}{2}k}V_{k}^{t}(z)=(1+zt/\sqrt{c})V_{k}^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}z).

Now use (
30
) to get

\begin{split}\|V^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{1/2}x)\|^{2}&=q^{-1}\frac{t^{2}}{c}\frac{(1-qt^{-2})^{2}}{1+qx/\sqrt{c}t}\|V^{t}(x)\|^{2},\\
\|V^{q^{-1/2}t}(q^{-1/2}x)\|^{2}&=\frac{t^{2}}{c}\frac{(1-qt^{-2})^{2}}{1+tx/\sqrt{c}}\|V^{t}(x)\|^{2}.\end{split}

The result now follows by substituting in
\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(x)=\|V^{t}(x)\|^{-1}V_{k}^{t}(x)
for
x\in\sigma(L)
.
∎

Appendix B
Calculations for the operator-algebraic quantum group action

In this section, we calculate the action of some of the operators of the coordinate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and the quantum universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{su}(1,1))
. Most of these calculations are somewhat tedious, and make use of the
q
-difference relations of the special functions as given/derived in Appendix
A
.

B.1
Actions in the quantum Iwasawa decomposition

Let us first calculate the action of the coordinate algebra on the basis elements
g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
of
L_{q}^{2}(\mathrm{SU}(1,1)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2})
as given in (
97
). We follow a similar derivation as given in
KS2001
. First, let
x=q^{2k}
or
x=-t^{-2}q^{k2}
, i.e.
e(x)=1
, and consider the element
\alpha_{\infty,t}^{*}=\alpha^{*}+qt^{-1}\gamma
(
91
). By Lemma
A.15
, we have

\begin{split}\alpha_{\infty,t}^{*}.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(tx;1;q^{2})(\alpha^{*}+qt^{-1}\gamma).\delta_{n,q^{-2k},m+2k}^{\pm}\\
&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sqrt{1+q^{-2k}}~\tilde{V}_{k+1}^{t}(tx;1;q^{2})+t^{-1}q^{1-k}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(tx;1;q^{2})\right)\delta_{n-1,q^{-2k},m+1+2k}^{\pm}\\
&=-\sqrt{1+q^{2}x}~g^{\pm,q^{-1}t}_{n-1,q^{2}x,m+1}.\end{split}

(1)

This also gives the action of
\alpha_{\infty,qt}
by taking the adjoint.
For
x=-q^{-2k}
, i.e.
e(x)=-1
, one can assume that
\alpha_{\infty,qt}.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}=C\cdot g_{n+1,q^{-2}x,m-1}^{\pm,qt}
, which can be motivated by the commutation relation of
\alpha_{st}
with the spherical element
\rho_{st}
(
94
). We get

\begin{split}-Cp_{0}^{(qt)^{-2}}(qtx;q^{2})&=\langle\alpha_{\infty,qt}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m},\delta^{\pm}_{n+1,q^{-2},m+1}\rangle=\langle g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m},\alpha_{\infty,qt}^{*}.\delta^{\pm}_{n+1,q^{-2},m+1}\rangle\\
&=-q^{-1}t^{-1}p_{0}^{t^{-2}}(tq^{2}x;q^{2}).\end{split}

(2)

Therefore, we have
C=q^{-1}t^{-1}p_{0}^{t^{-2}}(tq^{2}x;q^{2})/p_{0}^{(qt)^{-2}}(qtx;q^{2})
, which can be calculated directly using the definition of the Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomial (
26
). Analogously, the above steps can be done for
\gamma_{\infty,t}
. The actions on the basis elements
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
(
101
) can now be calculated directly and are given by (
105
).

Let us now calculate the left action of
K
on the basis
g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}
(
97
). For
x=q^{2k}
or
x=-t^{-2}q^{-2k}
such that
e(x)=1
, one finds

\begin{split}K.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}&=q^{\frac{m}{2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{k}\tilde{V}_{k}^{t}(tx;q;q^{2})\delta^{\pm}_{n,q^{-2k},m+2k}\\
&=q^{\frac{m}{2}}(tx)^{-1}\left[\sqrt{1+t^{2}x}~g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,q^{-1}t}-\sqrt{q^{-2}+x}~g_{n,q^{2}x,m}^{\pm,q^{-1}t}\right],\end{split}

(3)

where we used Lemma
A.16
in the second equality. For
x=-q^{-2k}
such that
e(x)=-1
one can use Lemma
A.13
to find a similar expression. Also, since
K^{*}=K
, one can obtain a second expression for the action of
K
by taking the adjoint of the above result. In particular, the action of
K
is given by

\begin{split}K.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}&=q^{\frac{m}{2}}(tx)^{-1}\left[\sqrt{e(x)(1+t^{2}x)}e.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,q^{-1}t}-\sqrt{e(x)(q^{-2}+x)}g_{n,q^{2}x,m}^{\pm,q^{-1}t}\right]\\
&=q^{\frac{m}{2}}(tx)^{-1}\left[\sqrt{e(x)(q^{-2}+t^{2}x)}e.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,qt}-\sqrt{e(x)(1+x)}g_{n,q^{-2}x,m}^{\pm,qt}\right].\end{split}

(4)

Combining these two expressions then gives the
q
-difference equation for
K^{2}
, which is given by

\begin{gathered}\frac{1}{2}e(x)q^{-m-1}K^{2}.g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}=A(x)g_{n,q^{-2}x,m}^{\pm,t}+B(x)g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}+A(q^{2}x)g_{n,q^{2}x,m}^{\pm,t},\\
A(x)=-\frac{1}{2}(tx)^{-2}\sqrt{(1+t^{2}x)(1+x)},\quad B(x)=\frac{1}{2}q^{-1}(tx)^{-2}(1+q^{-2}+(1+t^{2})x),\end{gathered}

(5)

where one can see that the value of
t
remains unchanged. Next, let us calculate the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
on
g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
. From the definition of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
(
90
), it follows that

2\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}=q^{1/2}(q-q^{-1})EK-q^{-1/2}(q-q^{-1})FK-(s+s^{-1})K^{2}.

(6)

It can be directly calculated from (
84
) that

\begin{split}&q^{1/2}(q-q^{-1})\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}.EK\\
&=\mp e(x)\sqrt{1\pm q^{-n-m-2}x^{-1}}\delta^{\pm}_{n+2,x,m}+q^{-n-1}e(x)\sqrt{1+x^{-1}}\delta^{\pm}_{n+2,q^{-2}x,m}.\end{split}

(7)

For
x=q^{2k}
or
x=-t^{-2}q^{-2k}
such that
e(x)=1
, we can act with
EK
on the basis
g_{n,x,m}^{\pm,t}
(
97
), which gives

\begin{split}&q^{1/2}(q-q^{-1})g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.EK\\
&=\mp\sqrt{1+q^{-n-m-2}}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m}+q^{-n-1}\sum_{k}q^{k-1}\sqrt{1+q^{2-2k}}\tilde{V}_{k-1}^{t}(tx;1;q^{2})\delta^{\pm}_{n+1,q^{-2k},m-2+2k},\\
&=\mp\sqrt{1+q^{-n-m-2}}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m}-tq^{-n-2}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m-2}+q^{-n-2}q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\sqrt{1+t^{2}x}K.g^{\pm,q^{-1}t}_{n+2,x,m-2},\end{split}

(8)

where we used Lemma
A.15
in the last step. A similar derivation can be done when
e(x)=-1
. Here, it can be noted that the actions of the coordinate algebra (
105
) define new relations on the Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials, which also appear in the right action of
EK
. Note here that the above equation still has a remaining left action of
K
. Writing out this action using (
4
), we get

\begin{split}&q^{1/2}(q-q^{-1})g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.EK\\
&=\mp e(x)\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m-2}}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m}+e(x)q^{-n-2}t^{-1}x^{-1}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m+2}\\
&+q^{-n}x^{-1}e\gamma_{\infty,qt}^{*}\alpha_{\infty,qt}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.\end{split}

(9)

A similar expression can be found for the right action of
q^{-1/2}(q-q^{-1})FK
by taking the adjoint. Now, we can substitute in
\tilde{\rho}_{st}
as given in (
107
) to find that the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
on basis element
g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
is given by

\begin{split}g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}&=\left(\mp\frac{1}{2}e(x)\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m-2}}g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m}+\frac{1}{2}q^{-n-2}t^{-1}x^{-1}e(x)g^{\pm,t}_{n+2,x,m-2}\right)\\
&+\left(\mp\frac{1}{2}e(x)\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m}}g^{\pm,t}_{n-2,x,m}+\frac{1}{2}q^{-n}t^{-1}x^{-1}e(x)g^{\pm,t}_{n-2,x,m+2}\right)\\
&-q^{-n-1}(tx)^{-1}\tilde{\rho}_{st}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.\end{split}

(10)

Lastly, we can calculate the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
on the basis elements
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
(
101
). Upon this action, the terms in the brackets can be simplified using the
q
-difference relation as given in Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.8
for the
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions (if
e(x)=1
) or the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (if
e(x)=-1
), respectively. Applying these relations then gives (
106
).

We can also calculate the action of the Casimir element in the Iwasawa decomposition. Firstly, let us split up the action of the Casimir element (
129
) as

\tilde{\Omega}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}=\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}+\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}+\tilde{\Omega}^{R}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m},

(11)

where

\begin{split}\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}&=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1+x^{-1})(1\pm q^{-n-m}x^{-1})}\delta_{n,q^{-2}x,m}^{\pm},\\
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}&=-\frac{1}{2}(q^{-m}\pm q^{-n})q^{-1}x^{-1}\delta^{\pm}_{n,x,m}\end{split}

(12)

and
\tilde{\Omega}^{R}=(\tilde{\Omega}^{L})^{*}
. Also, we will write the basis elements
g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
(
97
) as

g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}=\sum_{k}g_{k}^{t}(x)\delta^{\pm}_{n,e(x)q^{-2k},m+2k},

(13)

where
g_{k}^{t}
corresponds to the Al-Salam-Carlitz II polynomials or the Ciccoli-Koelink-Koornwinder functions for
e(x)=-1
or
e(x)=1
, respectively. The action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{L}
on this basis can be calculated to be equal to

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}\\
&=\sum_{k}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1+e(x)q^{2k})(1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m})}g_{k}^{t}(x)\delta^{\pm}_{n,e(x)q^{-2(k+1)},m-2+2(k+1)}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m}}\sum_{k}\left(tq^{k-1}\sqrt{e(x)(t^{-1}+x)}g_{k}^{q^{-1}t}(x)-tq^{-1}g_{k}^{t}(x)\right)\delta^{\pm}_{n,e(x)q^{-2k},m-2+2k}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m}}\left(-tq^{-1}g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m-2}+q^{-\frac{m}{2}}t\sqrt{e(x)(t^{-2}+x)}K.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m-2}(q^{-1}t)\right),\end{split}

(14)

where in the second step we used an identity for
g_{k}^{t}(x)
that can be derived from the action of
\beta_{\infty,q^{-1}t}
on
g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}
. By evaluating the left action of
K
as given in (
4
), and also by direct calculation of the action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}
, we have

\begin{split}\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}&=\frac{1}{2}t^{-1}x^{-1}\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m}}\left(q^{-1}g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m-2}-\sqrt{(1+t^{2}x)(1+x)}g^{\pm,t}_{n,q^{-2}x,m-2}\right),\\
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}&=-\frac{1}{2}e(x)q^{-1}q^{-m}g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}\mp\frac{1}{2}e(x)q^{-1}q^{-n-m}K^{2}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}.\end{split}

One can now write out the action of
K^{2}
using (
5
) and also substitute in the action of
\tilde{Y}^{L}_{t}
as given in (
99
). This now allows us to recombine terms to rewrite the Casimir action with

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}=\left(\mp\frac{1}{2}q^{-1-n}(tx)^{-1}(1+q^{-2}+(1+t^{2})x)-q^{-1}t^{-1}x^{-1}\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}\right).g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}\\
&\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.g^{\pm,t}_{n,x,m}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}(tx)^{-1}\sqrt{(1+t^{2}x)(1+x)}\left(\sqrt{1\pm e(x)q^{-n-m}}g^{\pm,t}_{n,q^{-2}x,m-2}\mp q^{-n}t^{-1}x^{-1}g^{\pm,t}_{n,q^{-2}x,m}\right).\end{split}

(15)

This action can now be evaluated on the basis
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
as given in (
101
). Since
\tilde{Y}_{t}^{L}
acts diagonally on this basis, the action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}
is immediately clear. Moreover, when one acts with
\tilde{\Omega}^{L}
on
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
, the term in the brackets an be simplified using the relation of Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.8
for the little
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions (if
e(x)=1
) or the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (if
e(x)=-1
), respectively. After combining all the terms, one finds that the action of the Casimir element in the Iwasawa decomposition is given by

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Omega}.\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}=A(x)\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,q^{-2}x,\xi}+B(x)\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}+A(q^{2}x)\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,q^{2}x,\xi},\\
A(x)=-\frac{1}{2}e(x)\sqrt{(1+x^{-1})(1+t^{-2}x^{-1})(1\pm\xi^{-1}(tx)^{-1}q^{-n})(1\pm\xi(tx)^{-1}q^{-n})},\\
B(x)=-\frac{1}{2}(2t^{-1}\mu(\xi)\pm t^{-2}x^{-1}(1+q^{-2}+(1+t^{2})x)q^{-n})q^{-1}x^{-1}.\end{gathered}

(16)

B.2
Actions in the quantum Gauss decomposition

Let us now calculate some of the actions of operators in the quantum Gauss decomposition. The methods used here are completely analogous to the ones used in Section
B.1
, and we will therefore be a bit more brief about the derivations.

Firstly, we comment on the action of the coordinate algebra. The action of the coordinate algebra on the basis in the Gauss decomposition can be calculated in a completely analogous way to how this action in the Iwasawa decomposition (
105
) was derived in Appendix
B.1
. In particular, after acting with
\alpha_{s,qt}=\alpha_{\infty,qt}-s^{-1}\gamma_{\infty,qt}
on the basis elements
h^{\pm,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}
as given in (
111
), one recognises the relations for the little
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions or the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials as given in Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.8
. Applying these relations to action on the basis elements
\delta^{\pm,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
then gives the result of (
124
).

Next, we will calculate the action of the universal enveloping algebra in the Gauss decomposition. Note that
\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}.K^{-1}=q^{\frac{n}{2}}\delta^{\pm,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}
. If we now consider the right action of
K^{-1}
on basis elements
h^{\pm,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}
as given in (
111
), one recognises the
q
-difference relations for the
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions (if
e(x)=1
) and the Al-Salam-Chihara polynomials (if
e(x)=-1
) as given in Lemma
A.5
or Lemma
A.11
, respectively. For
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st})\setminus[-1,1]
this therefore gives

\begin{gathered}h_{n,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}.K^{-1}=A_{s}(y)h_{n,qy,\xi}^{\pm,(q^{-1}s,t)}+A_{s}(y^{-1})h_{n,q^{-1}y,\xi}^{\pm,(q^{-1}s,t)},\\
A_{s}(y)=q^{\frac{n}{2}}\sqrt{-e(x)\kappa(y)qst^{\kappa(y)}y\frac{(1-qs^{-1}ty)(1-qs^{-1}t^{-1}y)}{(1-y^{2})(1-q^{2}y^{2})}}.\end{gathered}

(17)

Since
K
is self-adjoint, one can take the adjoint of the above action to obtain

h_{n,y,\xi}^{\pm,(st)}.K^{-1}=A_{qs}(q^{-1}y)h_{n,q^{-1}y,\xi}^{\pm,(qs,t)}+A_{qs}(q^{-1}y^{-1})h_{n,qy,\xi}^{\pm,(qs,t)}.

(18)

By combining these two actions, one now finds that the right action of
K^{-2}
is given by the
q
-difference equation

\begin{gathered}e(x)\kappa(y)q^{-n}h_{n,y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}.K^{-2}=A(y)h_{n,q^{-2}y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}+B(y)h_{n,y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)}+A(y^{-1})h_{n,q^{2}y,\xi}^{\varepsilon,(st)},\\
A(y)=t^{\kappa(y)}\sqrt{\frac{y^{2}(1-q^{-1}s^{\pm}t^{\pm}y)}{(1-y^{2})(1-q^{-2}y^{2})^{2}(1-q^{-4}y^{2})}},\quad B(y)=qt^{\kappa(y)}\frac{\mu(t)\mu(s)-\mu(s)\mu(y)}{\mu(y)^{2}-\mu(q)^{2}},\end{gathered}

(19)

where

(1-q^{-1}s^{\pm}t^{\pm}y)=(1-q^{-1}sty)(1-q^{-1}s^{-1}ty)(1-q^{-1}st^{-1}y)(1-q^{-1}s^{-1}t^{-1}y).

(20)

Note here that
\pm
does not refer to a variable in the basis element anymore. That is, we changed notations
\delta^{\pm}\to\delta^{\varepsilon}
, such that the notation of the actions can be ‘compactified’. We can now derive the action of the Casimir element on the basis
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,x,\xi}
. Firstly, we can split up the Casimir action in the Iwasawa decomposition (
16
) as

\tilde{\Omega}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}=\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}+\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}+\tilde{\Omega}^{R}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}.

where

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{m,x,\xi}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}e(x)\sqrt{(1+x^{-1})(1+t^{-2}x^{-1})(1+\varepsilon\xi^{-1}(tx)^{-1}q^{-n})(1+\varepsilon\xi(tx)^{-1}q^{-n})}\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,q^{-2}x,\xi},\\
&\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}=-\frac{1}{2}(2t^{-1}\mu(\xi)+\varepsilon t^{-2}x^{-1}(1+q^{-2}+(1+t^{2})x)q^{-n})q^{-1}x^{-1}\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n,x,\xi}.\end{split}

(21)

When acting on
h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}
, it can now be immediately seen that the action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}
can be rewritten in terms of the action of
K^{-2}
. In particular, one finds

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}^{C}.h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}\\
&=\varepsilon\frac{1}{2}q^{-1}(1+t^{-2})q^{-n}.h^{\pm,st}_{n,y,\xi}+(q^{-1}t^{-1}\mu(\xi)q^{-n}+\varepsilon\frac{1}{2}q^{-1}t^{-2}(1+q^{-2})q^{-2n})h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}.K^{-2}.\end{split}

(22)

For the action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{L}
, let us consider the case where
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st}|_{W})\setminus[-1,1]
. In this case, the action of
\tilde{\Omega}_{L}
on
h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}
is given by

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n,y,\xi}=\sum_{k}j_{k}(-\mu(y);q^{2}s^{-2},-qs^{-1}t^{-1};q^{2},-1)\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.\delta^{\varepsilon,(\infty,t)}_{n+2k,q^{-2k},\xi}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1+\varepsilon\xi^{-1}t^{-1}q^{-n})(1+\varepsilon\xi t^{-1}q^{-n})}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{k}\sqrt{(1+q^{2k})(q+t^{-2}q^{2k})}j_{k}(-\mu(y);q^{2}s^{-2},-qs^{-1}t^{-1};q^{2},-1)\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n-2+2(k+1),q^{-2(k+1)},\xi}.\end{split}

(23)

Here, one can now apply Lemma
A.3
and Corollary
A.4
for the little
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions to rewrite the term in the sum. One gets a similar expression when
\mu(y)\in\sigma(\tilde{\rho}_{st}|_{U,V})\setminus[-1,1]
, and one can use Lemma
A.9
and Lemma
A.10
to simplify the expression. In particular, the action of
\tilde{\Omega}^{L}
can be calculated to be equal to

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\Omega}^{L}.h_{n,y,\xi}^{\varepsilon.(st)}=A(y)h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n-2,q^{-2}y,\xi}+B(y)h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n-2,y,\xi}+A(y^{-1})h^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{n-2,q^{2}y,\xi},\\
A(y)=\frac{1}{2}q^{-2}y^{2}\sqrt{\frac{(1-q^{-1}s^{\pm}t^{\pm}y)(1+\varepsilon\xi^{\pm}t^{-1}q^{-n})}{(1-y^{2})(1-q^{-2}y^{2})(1-q^{-4}y^{2})}},\\
B(y)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(1+\varepsilon\xi^{\pm}t^{-1}q^{-n})}\frac{\mu(s)\mu(q)+\mu(t)\mu(y)}{\mu(y)^{2}-\mu(q)^{2}}.\end{gathered}

(24)

One can now finally consider the action of the Casimir element on the basis
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
. In particular, when writing out the actions of
\tilde{\Omega}^{C}
and
\tilde{\Omega}^{L}
as given above on basis elements
\delta^{\varepsilon,(st)}_{\eta,y,\xi}
, one will recognise the right action of
\tilde{Y}_{s}^{R}
. Moreover, the
q
-difference relations as given in Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.8
for the little
q^{2}
-Jacobi functions and Al-Salam-Chihara functions will appear. Analogous to the derivation of the action of the Iwasawa decomposition, the action of the Casimir element in the Gauss decomposition can then be derived to be given by (
130
).
